**The XP bar made the header need data it had never needed.** Lifetime XP is DERIVED from every stored run, not accumulated, which is what keeps a level from drifting out of step with the runs behind it — so the header cannot read a number, it has to fetch the history and recompute. Two consequences. It loads asynchronously and the bar simply appears when ready, so a slow or failed read costs the bar and never the name or the nav. And `loadOwnHistory` caches the promise per page load, because the profile page needs exactly the same rows: without it, opening your profile fetched a few hundred rows twice. A test asserts one fetch serves both, and another checks the displayed level equals recomputing from the same history.

**The hamburger holds the nav, not the nameplate.** Keeping the name and its bar outside the menu was a deliberate choice made before writing any of it: the bar should be glanceable, and the XP animation needs a visible target to fly into — a pill flying into a closed hamburger says nothing. The dropdown reuses the SAME buttons rather than rendering a second copy, because a duplicate set would need every `hidden` rule applied twice, and the Admin button being wrongly visible is a mistake this project has already made (§11n). A test asserts a non-admin still never sees Admin inside the open menu.

Visibility is owned by the media query, not by a `hidden` attribute, so the menu reappears on resize with no listener involved; only the open/closed state is a class.

The button itself is bare (owner follow-up: "just be the three lines... not the big white square"). Every other control in the bar is a bordered pill, and with no class styling of its own this one fell back to the UA's default button chrome — a grey bordered box. Background and border are now removed outright and the glyph takes `--accent`, the login button's green. Padding stays despite there being no visible box, because the tap target still has to be finger-sized. It also resets `letter-spacing`: `#site-header button` tracks its text, which on a single glyph is dead width on the right that pushes it off centre — the same trap the lone "?" on the help button hit.

**The XP pill flies between two elements in different parts of the document**, so like `.score-spark` it is fixed-position and appended to `<body>` — neither the result panel nor the header could contain it. Two details that were wrong first time:

It positions against the digits' INK, not the element box. `.score-total` is a full-width block, so "just right of it" put the pill out at the panel border rather than beside the number; a `Range` over the text contents measures the digits themselves. And because the pill is translated by -50% in both axes (so flight offsets can be computed between box centres), the `left` it is given is its CENTRE — half its width has to be added to clear the digits, and the result clamped so a wide number on a narrow screen cannot push it off-screen.

It is `--accent` green, matching the bar it flies into so the pill visibly belongs to its destination. The first attempt used `--selected`, which is the amber card-selection colour and read as "this is picked" — a different idea entirely.

It fires LAST, after the achievements branch, because achievements award XP of their own: showing the number earlier would show a figure that did not match what was earned.

**Verified** with 30 new browser assertions and 5 new unit tests. The browser ones cover the bar's fill matching its announced `aria-valuenow`, the level agreeing with a recomputation, one shared history fetch, the menu opening and closing (including on an outside tap) with Admin still hidden, the breakpoint behaving in both directions, and the pill appearing only after the fortune, beside the digits, inside the card, then cleaning itself up.

**Two existing suites needed updating, and the distinction mattered.** `hdr4-run` asserted the nameplate is the LAST child of the header's right side — an invariant added because the name used to shift depending on whether you were an admin. The hamburger now follows it in the DOM, which does not reintroduce that bug (the menu button exists at every width and is only *displayed* below the breakpoint, so it never varies per player), so the assertion was rewritten to test the actual intent: the nameplate follows all nav, only the menu button may follow it, and at desktop width it is the last visible slot. `mods-run` opened the friends panel at 460px, which is now below the breakpoint — it opens the menu first, exactly as a player on that width has to.

---

### 11u. The Bar Actually Filling, Level-Ups, and a Local Test Account ✅ built (owner: "i want the xp to show and then be animated and travel up to the xp bar and see the bar go up and flash highlight if it levels up... for the localhost test mode have the option to sign in as a fake account so i can test the new things like the xp")

**The bar was not animating, and the reason was a one-line habit.** §11t gave `.header-xp-fill` a `transition: width`, then updated it by calling `renderAuthSlot()` — which replaces the auth slot's `innerHTML`. That destroys the fill element and builds a new one already at its final width, and a brand-new node has nothing to transition FROM. The value was always correct; it simply appeared rather than moved. It now mutates the element already on screen, and a test asserts the fill node is never replaced mid-animation (`fillCount === 1`) alongside sampling the intermediate widths — the value check alone would have passed the whole time this was broken.

**A level-up runs the bar to full before starting the next one from empty.** Snapping straight to the new percentage would read as the bar going *backwards*, since crossing a level resets progress to near zero — the opposite of what just happened. So: fill to 100%, hold on a gold flash with the level number popping, reset to empty without animating, then fill to the real new position. The reset forces a layout read between setting `transition: none` and restoring it, because without that the browser coalesces both writes into a single no-op. It loops, since a big run can cross more than one level.

Under reduced motion the flash and the pop are dropped but the fill still moves: a progress bar that teleports is not a decoration, it is the feature.

**The fake account exists because levels are derived.** Exercising the bar, a level-up, or the XP flight otherwise needs a real account with a real history — which cannot be rewound, and at one run per day takes weeks to build. `state/test-account.js` fabricates a signed-in identity behind TWO independent gates: `?test` in the URL (which production links never carry, and which already gates the redeal panel) and a localStorage flag set deliberately from that panel. It never obtains a Supabase session, so it grants no database access at all — every read it serves is fabricated locally, and a real query would still face RLS as an anonymous caller. A test asserts that with the flag set but no `?test`, the page is simply signed out.

Its history is SYNTHESISED FROM RUNS, not stated as a total. That is what makes it worth having: `derivePlayerStats` recomputes XP from results, so a fabricated number would exercise a path production never takes. A run carrying only the base XP is worth exactly 100 — High Card, score 0 (grade Busted), no bonuses, rating 0 — so *n* of those plus one whose decision rating supplies the remainder (worth 0–100) hits any target exactly. A test checks five targets land on the nose.

**One thing the fake account needed that was not obvious.** Test mode deliberately never writes a real result, so the synthetic history regenerated identically after every run and the bar had nothing to move to — it looked signed in but was frozen. Completed runs now bank their XP into the fake total, which is what makes "play a hand and watch it fill" work at all. The panel also offers "set to 50 XP short of the next level", so a level-up is one run away rather than a matter of waiting.

**The first version shipped broken, and my own test hid it.** The owner reported the header still saying "Log In" with no bar. `getSession()` was returning the fake session perfectly — but Supabase's `onAuthStateChange` fires immediately on subscribe with `INITIAL_SESSION`, and since nobody is really signed in that value is `null`, which promptly overwrote it. The header believed the last thing it was told.

Every test had blocked esm.sh, on the entirely reasonable grounds that the fake account must work with no backend — which meant the subscription never fired and the clobber never happened. The condition that mattered was not "no backend" but "a live backend that says nobody is signed in", and nothing exercised it. `onAuthStateChange` now reports the fake session once and never subscribes, and a test stubs a reachable-but-signed-out Supabase to pin it; reverting the fix turns three assertions red and reproduces "Log In" exactly.

**The flight was invisible to anyone with reduced motion on**, which is how the owner saw it: "i dont want the xp to just appear and disappear, i want it to move up to where the xp bar is." The media query swapped the whole journey for a fade-in-place. Measured rather than guessed — sampling the pill's centre every frame gave 285px x / 874px y with the preference off, and 0px / 18px with it on.

That was the wrong call, and the same one already rejected for the XP bar's own fill: reduced motion means REDUCE, not remove, and the travel is not decoration here — it is the entire message, the thing that connects the number to the bar it feeds. The journey is now kept under the preference and the agitation taken out instead: no pop-scale, no shrink, a longer and entirely linear path. Both modes are now sampled frame by frame and required to move at least 200px and finish on the nameplate.

The flight also reads better generally: 1000ms rather than 780 (it can cover most of the viewport), opacity held to 85% of the way so the eye can follow it the whole distance, a gentler shrink, and an ease-in-out so it pulls away from the score and settles into the bar rather than sliding at a constant rate.

**Verified** with 24 new browser assertions covering both gates independently, the exact-XP synthesis, a signed-in fake account rendering a correct level with esm.sh blocked, the same with a live backend reporting no session, the bar passing through intermediate widths without its node being replaced, the level-up flash firing, the level advancing, and no leakage into a normal load.

---

### 11v. Support Footer ✅ built (owner: "add my buymeacoffee link at the very bottom... have a message like help support the page")

A site-wide `<footer>`, a sibling of `#app` rather than part of any view, so it sits below whichever page was rendered — board, profile, leaderboards or admin — without four copies to keep in step.

**A plain text link, not Buy Me a Coffee's branded button.** That button is a remote image, and this project loads no third-party assets at all beyond the single Supabase module — so it would be a new external request, one that ad blockers stop by default, leaving a broken image exactly where the ask was. A styled text link always renders. `rel="noopener"` alongside `target="_blank"`, since otherwise the opened tab gets a `window.opener` handle back to this one.

**`margin-top: auto` is what puts it at the bottom.** `body` is a `min-height: 100vh` flex column, so the last child absorbs the slack: on a short page (an empty profile, a signed-out leaderboard) the footer is pushed to the viewport bottom instead of floating mid-screen, and on a long one it simply follows the content. A test asserts exactly that on a page short enough not to scroll.

Deliberately quiet — muted, small, and last. It is an offer, not a call to action, and it should not compete with the game.

**The first version left ~73px of dead space above the link** (owner: "too much padding above link"), and measuring split it into two unrelated causes. Only one was padding: `#app`'s 3rem bottom padding, which existed to stop content butting against the viewport edge back when nothing followed it, was stacking with the footer's own 1.5rem. The footer now does that job, so `#app` drops to 1.25rem and the footer's top padding to 0.5rem — 73px down to 29px.

The other cause is not padding at all and is unchanged: on a page too short to scroll, `margin-top: auto` pushes the footer to the viewport bottom, so the gap is simply the empty page. Removing the auto margin would trade that for a footer stranded mid-screen with blank space beneath it, which is worse.

**Verified** with 17 browser assertions: present and below the content on all four routes, the correct URL, the copy explaining what it is for, `target`/`rel` set safely, and no third-party asset introduced by it.

---

### 11w. Discoverability, Progression Pacing, and Three Silent Failures ✅ built (owner: "google seo... xp easier to earn/levels scale better... dont show xp if not logged in... share button not showing wilds/correct card the wild should be... share button being blocked by some ad blockers... 'that sign in link didn't work' on mobile, 'importing a module script failed', also still not saving login on mobile... modifier didn't save from changing it in the site through the admin panel")

Seven items that turned out to share one theme: **a thing that appeared to work, silently didn't.** Four of them produced no error at all — not in the console, not in a test, not to the player.

**SEO for a page with an empty body.** The markup is built entirely by JavaScript, so a crawler's first pass sees a shell. Google runs JS on a second pass that can lag by days; Bing and every social unfurler largely don't. Everything a crawler can rely on therefore has to live in `<head>`: a description, Open Graph and Twitter cards, and `VideoGame` JSON-LD. `robots.txt` and `sitemap.xml` sit at the REPO ROOT, not in `/public` — `vercel.json` serves the repo root as the static root and rewrites only `/`, and `tools/dev-server.js` resolves the same way, so both are reachable at their required origin-root paths in dev and production alike. The sitemap lists exactly one URL: the query-param routes (`?profile`, `?leaderboard`) render nothing without a session, so listing them would put thin duplicates into the index competing with the homepage, which is also why `<link rel="canonical">` points at `/`.

**The first description was factually wrong, and shipped.** It claimed "everyone gets the same five cards" — the mechanic that was *deliberately removed* when hands became randomly dealt per player (`state/persistence.js:56`). Meta tags are the one part of the page nobody looks at while developing, so it was wrong in every link preview until the owner saw an unfurl. Chasing it down found the same false premise sitting in How to Play, phrased as "the only thing that separates a good day from a bad one is which cards you throw away" — true when everyone held identical cards, and now a way of telling a player who drew badly that they merely played worse. What IS shared on a given day is the Daily Modifier, the day number and the fortune vocabulary. The deal is not. The `<head>` now carries an accuracy note saying so, because the next person writing marketing copy will have no reason to suspect it.

**The level curve was the problem, not the XP.** Bands grew as `300 × L` forever, so level 20→21 alone cost 6,000 XP — about three weeks at the one run per day the game hands out by design (§9.2). A player's rate of progress is FIXED, so any super-linear curve eventually outruns them no matter how well they play; that doesn't read as "a long-term goal", it reads as the game quietly ending. Bands now widen by 150 and then **cap at 1,500**, so levelling is asymptotically steady — roughly one level every four to five days, forever. `XP_BASE_PER_RUN` rose 100 → 150 to lift the floor, since that is the only term that pays for a bad day. Because every new band is ≤ the old one at the same level, the retune can only move an existing player UP — the property that makes it safe to ship at all, given levels are DERIVED from `daily_plays` and everyone re-levels on their next load. A test pins it by comparing against the curve it replaced.

**XP shown to a logged-out player is a promise the game can't keep.** Anonymous play persists nothing, yet `revealXpGain` ran for everyone — and its flight target is the header nameplate, which for a logged-out visitor is the **Log In button**. The gain literally flew into a control saying the player has no account.

**The share text contradicted its own first line.** A wild is an ordinary dealt card carrying a flag, so its `rank`/`suit` are real, printable, and meaningless. `buildFinalHandText` printed them unconditionally, so a wild-completed Four of a Kind shared as "Four of a Kind" above five cards that visibly weren't — and the wild the player had seen on the board vanished, replaced by an unrelated face. Wilds now render `🃏→K♥`, showing both that a wild landed and what it played as. Fixing it surfaced two more in the same family: `logicalCardsFor` gave EVERY wild the first wild's substitution (harmless at ~1 wild per 100 hands, not at ~1 in 11), and the score-breakdown strip tested the legacy `rarity: 'joker'` shape only — with a local `isWild` shadowing the imported one, which is how the mismatch hid in plain sight.

**The Share button was named after a filter-list token.** `share-btn` was used as both the class AND the id, so either form of a generic cosmetic rule matched it. A blocker injects `display: none !important`, which beats `hidden = false` — the button simply never appeared and nothing errored. It is now `result-copy-btn`; nothing about it is a social widget, since it makes no network request at all. The rule to keep: **filter lists match selectors, not text**, so the visible label can still say Share.

**Three mobile auth symptoms, one cause.** The SDK was imported as `@supabase/supabase-js@2` — a major range. esm.sh picks a build target by **sniffing the User-Agent**, so an in-app webview (Gmail iOS, Outlook) or a new iOS Safari could be handed a build WebKit couldn't parse; a module that fails to PARSE rejects the dynamic import, and WebKit's message for that is exactly "Importing a module script failed." That single failure then explained all three reports: no client meant no session (so login "didn't save"), and `main.js` caught the parse error in the same `catch` as a bad token and reported it as **"that sign-in link didn't work"** — sending the owner hunting an expiry bug for weeks while the real fault was the CDN. Worse, the token was stripped from the URL either way, so the still-valid link was destroyed and every fresh email failed identically. Now: the version and `?target=` are pinned, a second independent CDN backs the first, `flowType: 'implicit'` is stated rather than inherited (a future 2.x defaulting to `pkce` would break the in-app-browser handoff silently, since PKCE needs a `code_verifier` from the browser that REQUESTED the link), and a distinct `SupabaseUnavailableError` lets the UI say the true thing and keep the token for a retry.

**The admin modifier saved correctly every time.** Reading the live table confirmed the write, the day key, and the override all agreed. Four surrounding mechanics were at fault: `render()` rebuilt every dropdown from stored config, so searching a username silently reverted an unsaved pick and the next Save wrote the OLD value and said "saved"; Save rebuilt the payload from only the visible 14 days, deleting every pin outside that window; `loadGameConfig` never rejects by design, so a failed read-back arrived as a valid-looking EMPTY config that showed success and armed a wipe; and the schedule never noticed the 19:00 rollover, so a tab left open filed pins under a dead day — the stored `updated_at` shows the owner editing fourteen seconds after one. A cache fix went with it: a failed config read was cached like a successful one, so a single blip at page load left the whole session on the rotation modifier while the pin sat in the database being ignored.

**Verified** with 534 unit tests (up from 519) and all 24 browser suites. Three of the new unit tests were confirmed to go RED with the fix reverted, as was the new logged-out XP coverage.

---

### 11x. Username Blocklist ✅ built (owner: "is there any username filter so people can't choose bad words like slurs or anything")

There wasn't. `^[A-Za-z0-9_]{3,20}$`, a length bound and a uniqueness check were the entire rule, so any slur that fit in twenty characters was accepted — and usernames are public, appearing on every leaderboard row, in friend search and on profile pages.

**It has to be in Postgres.** `profiles` is exposed straight to the browser and the anon key is public by design, so a JavaScript word check is bypassed by one `curl` against the REST endpoint. This is the same lesson migration 001 already learned about the character pattern, and the same shape of answer: the client check is UX, the database is the rule, both halves kept. A trigger rather than a CHECK constraint, because a CHECK cannot run a subquery — it could only hold a hardcoded regex, which would mean a deploy per word.

**`username` is user-updatable on their own row** (migration 004 grants UPDATE on exactly that column), so the trigger fires on UPDATE as well as INSERT. Guarding only INSERT would let anyone sign up clean and rename themselves a second later.

**The list has no read policy, and `game_config` was the wrong home for it.** That table is world-readable on purpose — every client reads the modifier schedule from it — so putting the list there would have published it. `username_blocklist` has RLS enabled and *no policies at all*, so a direct REST read returns `200 []` for every role including admins — the rows are invisible, though the table's existence is not hidden, which is what RLS does and is not the same as refusing the request. Only the `security definer` functions can see the contents, and `admin_list_blocked_words` correctly answers anon with `401 permission denied`. Both verified against the live database after the migration ran.

**Matching folds the evasion, which is where the difficulty actually is.** Case, leetspeak (`0→O`, `1→I`, `4→A`, `5→S`, `@→A`, `$→S`) and separator padding are all normalised away before comparison, so `5L_UR` and `slur` are one word. That folding is lossy on purpose, and lossy folding creates false positives — hence three tiers:

| Tier | Meaning | For |
|---|---|---|
| `substring` | blocked anywhere in the name | terms no innocent word contains |
| `exact` | blocked only as the whole name | words with innocent superstrings — `ASS` in `CLASSIC`, `COON` in `RACCOON`, `RAPE` in `GRAPE` |
| `allow` | exemption, checked first, wins outright | a real false positive, fixable as an admin action rather than a code change |

**The two tiers need DIFFERENT folding, which only became obvious once the live function was probed.** Migration 012 used one normaliser that translated the digits with leetspeak meanings (`4→A`, `1→I`) and deleted the rest (`2`, `6`, `9`). That produced a rule no player could infer — `Card_le_42` allowed, `Card_le_99` blocked, because only the second collapsed to exactly `CARDLE` — and it contradicted what `exact` is documented to mean, since `ASS9` is plainly not the whole name. The obvious repair, keeping all digits, opens an evasion hole instead: `N9I9G9G9E9R` stops folding to `NIGGER` and walks past the substring tier. So 013 splits them, which is what the tiers meant all along: **substring strips every non-letter** (it asks "does this word appear in here, however disguised"), **exact keeps leftover digits** (it asks "is the whole name this word", and a name with extra characters is by definition not). Separators are stripped in both, so `A_S_S` is still caught. A substring pattern containing a digit is now refused at insert time rather than stored as something that could never match.

**The refusal says nothing about why.** Naming the matched word would confirm what is on a list deliberately kept unreadable, would read as the site repeating a slur back at whoever typed it, and is the wrong message for someone who tripped a false positive and has done nothing wrong. `"NAME" isn't available` covers all three. The `username_available` RPC gives live feedback while typing for the same reason it returns one boolean and not a reason: a caller cannot distinguish "blocked" from "taken", so the list is not enumerable through it. Whether a name is taken is already public, so collapsing the two leaks nothing new.

**The client check fails OPEN.** An unreachable backend must not be able to refuse every name and lock new players out of signing up — the trigger is the real enforcement, so failing open costs a warning, while failing closed would cost the sign-up.

**`admin_rename_player` is the other half of the fix**, and was missing entirely. Until now the only response to an offensive name already stored was `admin_delete_player`, which also destroys that player's whole history — wildly out of proportion to a name. The blocklist trigger fires on the rename too, so an admin cannot land someone on a blocked name by accident.

**The starter list is deliberately short.** This file is committed to a public repository, and a comprehensive slur dictionary sitting in it is both unpleasant and largely pointless — anyone determined probes the live form regardless. What is seeded is the unambiguous core; the useful list is the one that grows in response to what people actually try, which is what the admin editor is for, and words added there never enter the repo.

**Verified** by a self-test inside the migration itself: sixteen cases covering plain hits, leetspeak and padding evasion, and the Scunthorpe guard (`CLASSIC`, `BASS_PLAYER`, `GRAPEVINE`, `RACCOON`, `ASSISTANT` must all survive), each printing `ok` or `FAIL`. That is where the verification lives because `npm test` has no Postgres — the matching rules are the part most likely to be subtly wrong and cannot be exercised from Node, so the migration checks itself when run. The JavaScript half is covered by unit tests.

---

### 11y. Security, Performance and Correctness Audit ✅ built (owner: "dive deep into any inefficient code, anything outdated, or any security problems that might occur and fix or note them")

A full sweep of the source tree. The findings clustered, and the clusters are more instructive than the individual bugs.

**Two authorization holes in the database, both the same shape as ones already fixed.** The `friendships` UPDATE policy allowed *either* party to update and had no column grant, so (a) a requester could accept their own outgoing request and appear in a stranger's friends list uninvited, and (b) — worse — because an omitted `WITH CHECK` defaults to the `USING` expression, the addressee of any request could rewrite `requester_id` to an arbitrary user id and then accept it, **fabricating a friendship with someone who never touched the site**. Neither is reachable through the UI; both are reachable through the REST endpoint, which does not care what the UI offers. Separately, uppercase usernames were a client-side convention only — the CHECK still accepted `[A-Za-z]`, and the unique index is case-sensitive, so a direct call could store `carson` beside `CARSON`: two rows indistinguishable to a player and invisible to every normalized lookup. This is the third and fourth time this project has needed the rule that **a row policy is never sufficient on its own** (after `daily_plays.seed` and `profiles.is_admin`).

**A deploy config silently undid an RLS design.** `vercel.json` sets `outputDirectory: "."`, so the repo root is the static root and every file in it is fetchable. Verified against production: `/supabase/migrations/012-username-blocklist.sql` returned 200 with the full blocklist seed list, and `/DESIGN.md` returned 392 KB of internal notes. §11x argues at length that the availability check must return a bare boolean so the word list "cannot be recited" — and the list was sitting in a world-readable `.sql` file the whole time. `.vercelignore` now excludes `supabase/`, `tests/`, `tools/` and the design docs. `src/` stays served deliberately: with no build step, the source *is* the bundle.

**board.js was the one UI module with no `escapeHtml` at all**, and it had grown two live injection paths. The fortune word bank used to be repo-authored literals — a comment in the file still said so — but §11g made it admin-editable through `game_config`, and `validateWordBank` only checks "non-empty string, no internal period". A phrase containing `</select><img src=x onerror=…>` renders into the story picker in **every player's browser** after every run, so one compromised admin account becomes arbitrary JavaScript in every session, including the Supabase token in `localStorage`. The second path is the stored `result` jsonb, which is player-written with no shape validation — self-XSS today because only your own result renders here, but the cross-user renderers already escape and this was one refactor from joining them. **The stale reassurance was part of the bug**: it told every reader there was nothing to check.

**Two real gameplay bugs, both found by reading rather than by anyone reporting them.**

*The scorer and the proof strip disagreed about which run won.* An Ace sits at both ends of the run scan, so `A♠ 2♥ 3♦ Q♣ K♠` contains two runs of three. `runLengthAndHighCard` kept the first (A-2-3) while `contributingIndices` highlighted the last (Q-K-A). The player was shown Q K A called out as the winning run and paid **473 points for A-2-3 instead of the 781 Q-K-A is worth** — and a rare card sitting in Q/K/A granted the whole-hand multiplier for a run the score had not come from. Two functions answering the same question with opposite tie-breaks.

*Double or Nothing handed out the game's "you played perfectly" rewards for free.* The wager forces `maxDiscards = 0` and locks in immediately, so the solver returns exactly one option and `best === worst`. `optimalDiscardBonus` short-circuits that case to the full **200 points** with a badge reading "your discard matched the mathematically best play" — for a round containing no discard — and `decisionRating` compares the score against the same five cards, giving exactly 1.0, which pinned Skill at 100% and unlocked `flawless`. Roughly one day in thirteen. Both are now *suppressed* rather than zeroed: the player did not play badly either, and 0% would be as untrue as 100%.

**A 4× solve speedup hiding in one line.** The two-wild branch of the wild search iterated all four suits for both wilds — 52×52 = 2,704 evaluations — while the one-wild branch right above it already pruned to 13. The pruning argument holds identically for two wilds (a flush needs five of a suit; two wilds supply at most two; so the three fixed cards must already share one), and three random cards share a suit only ~5% of the time. Since `ev-solver` walks the whole discard space, **0.3% of hands were consuming ~80% of a solve**. Confirmed behaviour-preserving by differential-testing 4,000 random two-wild hands against the exhaustive search — zero divergence, including the 260 flush-reachable cases.

**Verified** with 543 unit tests (up from 537) and all 24 browser suites. The SQL findings were confirmed against the live database before writing the fix, and the deploy exposure was confirmed by fetching the files from production.

---

### 11z. Server-Authoritative Scoring ✅ built (owner: "how do we go about fixing the daily_plays.result issue?")

§11y found that `daily_plays.result` was jsonb written wholesale by the client. RLS restricted which *row* a player could touch and nothing restricted what they could put in it — while `leaderboard_top_scores` reads the total straight out of that blob. Any signed-in player could PATCH their own row with an arbitrary number and own every board.

**The seed was always the answer.** It is claimed before a card is dealt, it is already write-once (`schema.sql` grants UPDATE on `result` alone), and `dealHand(seed)` is a pure function — same seed, same 52 cards, same rarity and wild flags. The server has always been able to reproduce the hand. It just had nowhere to run the check.

**The purity boundary is what made this cheap.** `src/core/` has never been allowed to touch the network or the DOM, so `verifyAndScoreRun` and everything it imports run unmodified under Deno. The Edge Function imports the *real* scorer rather than a second implementation — and a second implementation would have drifted from the browser's the first time either changed, which is the failure mode that makes most anti-cheat worse than none.

**Discarding is modelled as an ordered list of rounds**, because that is what the board actually does: Second Wind rebinds the hand after round one *and* advances the draw pile, so the stored `originalHand` on that day is the post-round-one hand. One code path then covers an ordinary day (one round), Second Wind (two), and a Double or Nothing wager (one round of zero) instead of three special cases. Round one's discards are now recorded for exactly this reason — without them the server could not reproduce what it was handed.

**The expensive part is bounded rather than verified.** `optimalDiscardBonus` needs the full exhaustive EV solve — the "Crunching the odds…" wait, millions of evaluations — and re-running it per submission would be the single costliest thing the backend does. It caps at 200 points against a table where a Royal Flush is 54,800,000, so the client's `evContext` is accepted and its blast radius is a rounding error. A *degenerate* context is discarded rather than honoured, since `bestEV === worstEV` short-circuits to the full bonus — the same trap §11y found in the wager path. `decisionRating`, `meters`, `personalityId` and `newlyUnlocked` are likewise clamped or filtered to known ids, never trusted raw.

**Rollout is ordered, not a cutover.** The client falls back to the direct write while the function is missing, so deploying in the wrong order cannot cost a player their run; migration 015 keeps the revoke commented out and gated behind a query that proves the function is actually writing (`result->>'verified'`). The rollback is one `grant`.

**Verified** by 15 new unit tests, the load-bearing one being that the verifier reproduces the client's total **exactly across 300 random seeds and discard choices**. That direction matters more than the cheating it prevents: if the two ever disagree, every honest player's score is silently rewritten on submit. The refusal cases (over-discarding, duplicate indices, out-of-range positions, discarding a locked card, extra rounds, degenerate EV contexts) are covered too. 558 unit tests and all 24 browser suites pass.

**Not verified here:** the Edge Function itself has never been executed — neither the Supabase CLI nor Deno is available in this environment. Its logic is the tested `verifyAndScoreRun`; what is unproven is the HTTP shell, the auth handshake, and whether the CLI bundles imports that reach outside `supabase/functions/` into `src/core/`. If it refuses, the fix is a symlink or a copy step — never a second scorer.

---

### 11aa. Daily Standing ✅ built (owner: "i want a thing like rngdle has where it says top/bottom 49% or whatever you score that day compared to others, have it be different colors based on the number")

A coloured chip under the grade: **TOP 3%**. Just the percentage — the field size and exact placing sit in the tooltip rather than the chip (owner: no "of xxx today"), because the denominator turned a one-glance badge into something to read. The score alone cannot answer "was that any good?" — 3,157 means nothing in isolation. The grade ladder (§11i) rates the hand against every hand that *could* exist; this rates it against the hands that actually turned up, and the two routinely disagree: a modest hand can top a quiet day.

**Counted in Postgres, not the browser.** The obvious implementation fetches today's rows and counts locally, and it is wrong twice over: every row carries the full stored `result` (hands, bonuses, meters — several KB each, growing with the player count forever) so a browser would download the entire day's play to compute one integer, and it would hand every player a complete dump of everyone else's hands. `daily_standing()` returns two integers. `security invoker` like the leaderboard functions, since it reads exactly what the caller may already read under `daily_plays`' own policy.

**TOP for the better half, BOTTOM for the worse.** "TOP 90%" is a euphemism nobody reads as bad news; the same placing said from the shorter end is honest and more informative. Colour is keyed on percentile *from the top* rather than on which word the label uses — otherwise TOP 50% and BOTTOM 50%, adjacent placings, would look like opposites.

**Nothing is shown below a five-run field.** Percentages of a tiny sample are noise dressed as information: first of three is "TOP 33%", and the same run reads differently an hour later purely because more people showed up. Worse, with two players the loser is always BOTTOM 100%, which reads as a verdict on them rather than on a two-person sample.

**Recomputed on every view, not frozen at lock-in.** The field grows all day, so a run that led at noon is mid-table by evening. Showing the stale noon figure would be a lie the player could disprove by opening the leaderboard.

**The screenshot caught what the test did not.** Both the grade and the chip were `inline-flex`, so the chip flowed onto the *same line* and sat overlapping the grade — while an assertion reading `chip.top >= grade.top` passed happily, because 622 ≥ 616. The fix is `display: flex` + `width: fit-content` (a block-level box takes its own line and still centres on auto margins); the assertion now requires the chip's top to clear the grade's **bottom**. Filled pill with white text rather than tinted text, because the default theme is light and dark is the media query — a filled pill carries its own contrast and looks identical either way. Every tier fill clears 4.5:1 against white (computed: 5.1, 6.6, 7.7, 6.1, 8.0).

**Verified** by 9 unit tests on the pure placing logic and 15 browser assertions covering both render paths, all five tiers, the too-small field, and the `[hidden]` trap. 567 unit tests and all 25 browser suites pass.

---

### 11ab. Click-to-View Hand Breakdowns ✅ built (owner: "i would like the option to see peoples hands and the breakdown of them but only when you click on it. like for example in the leaderboards or in the players profile")

The leaderboard already showed the hand somebody won with, and the profile already listed your past hands, but both were dead ends: five cards and a number, with no way to ask *how*. Clicking either now opens the full RNGDLE-style badge breakdown — every bonus, its description, and the card-proof strip — plus what they were dealt and which cards they threw.

**Three surfaces, not two** (owner follow-up: "only thing i wanna add is to be able to click it on the best hand in the profile too"). The Best Hand plaque was the obvious omission once the history rows below it were clickable — it is the one run a player most wants explained, and it sat inert directly above five rows that now weren't. `stats.bestRun` was already the whole stored blob, so the only thing missing was its date: `derivePlayerStats` recorded which run was best and not which day it came from. Added as `bestRunDate` on the stat rather than recovered in the UI by matching the result object back against `history` — that works only because they are the same reference, which is exactly the kind of thing that silently stops being true. The test that matters pins it against the *latest* run rather than the best one, because the assignment drifting outside the `if` is invisible whenever the best run happens to be the most recent.

**This would not have been shippable under the original design, and checking that was the first thing worth doing.** §2 specified one seed per day, hashed from the date, identical for everyone. Under that rule the stored blob's `originalHand` and `discardIndices` are a complete solution to today's puzzle, so opening the top run before drawing would hand a player the exact deal and the winning line — the same leak migration 006 wrote its `result is not null` clause to prevent for unfinished rows. The owner's answer was that the game no longer works that way, and the code agrees: `claimDailyPlay` generates a `freshSeed()` per player. So the gate was designed, priced, and then not built. **§2 was still asserting the shared seed** — §11w had corrected that same false claim in the meta description and How to Play a batch earlier and never traced it back to the section they were written from. A stale design doc is not a documentation problem when the staleness is what a security decision hangs on.

**The breakdown renderer moved rather than being copied.** `buildScoreBadges`/`badgeCardHtml`/`miniCardStripHtml` were private to `board.js`; three surfaces render them now. `score-breakdown.js` is the shared home, and the pure-string-builder discipline that let the *server* import the real scorer (§11z) is what let a UI module be lifted out and unit-tested in Node at all — no DOM, no timers, so 9 tests cover it directly. The one behavioural wrinkle worth naming: `buildScoreBadges` returns **ascending**, because that is the reveal order the animation depends on, while every static caller wants biggest-first. That `.reverse()` was about to be written twice, so `breakdownListHtml()` owns it once and a test pins the descending order — a dropped reverse would show the breakdown upside down with no error and no failing render.

**The leaderboard fetches, the profile doesn't.** The profile already pulls whole rows, so a click costs zero requests. The board's RPC returns only a score and a final hand, so it needs a second read — deliberately *not* a wider RPC, because the blob is several KB and widening it would put 25 on the wire on every tab switch to serve the at most one anybody opens. Same arithmetic as §11aa's daily standing, pointing the other way: there the fix was to send less, here it is to send it later. The modal opens on a loading line and fills in, so a slow network reads as loading rather than as a dead click, and it guards on `body.isConnected` because the player can close it mid-flight.

**A `<button>` on the hand, not a click handler on the row.** The row already contains the profile anchor; making the whole row activatable would have nested two destinations in one control and left the keyboard unable to choose between them. The hand is the affordance — you click the thing you want to look at. The real risk was that promoting a grid cell from `<span>` to `<button>` would break the column alignment §11n fought for, so that is what got measured: with a 54,800,000 score and a 21-character username in the same board, all five rows' cards still start at the same x. The career board keeps a plain span — no hand, nothing to open, and its footer hint drops the "or a hand" clause rather than advertising something that isn't there.

**A dev tool that had quietly stopped covering the feature.** The fake test account's fixture (§11u) carried only the handful of score fields the profile's stat tiles read, so `buildScoreBadges` threw on it and the modal opened empty. The guard did its job — one malformed blob loses its own breakdown instead of taking down the board listing it, which is the behaviour that matters when these rows come from a database and now render on other players' screens — but §11u's own rule is that a dev tool has to actually move. The fixture is now the complete `scoreRun()` shape, every value zeroed, so the XP arithmetic it is built around is untouched.

**It still cannot demo the Best Hand plaque, and that is left alone deliberately.** Every fake run scores 0, so `stats.bestScore` is falsy and `bestRunHtml` correctly renders nothing. Fixing it means giving a run a real score, which adds a grade term to `xpForRun` and breaks the exact-XP targeting `testAccountHistory` is built around — a real change to a carefully-balanced dev tool, in service of a demo. Recorded as a known limit instead, with the note that anything verifying the plaque locally needs a stubbed history rather than the fake account.

**Local testing needed no code at all, which was worth finding out before writing any.** The owner could not reach either surface on localhost because sign-in failed there — Redirect URLs did not list `http://localhost:8080/**`, and the magic-link template hardcodes `.SiteURL` so the email points at production regardless. Both are documented in `supabase/email-templates/magic-link.html`'s own setup notes. But neither needed changing: `verifySignInLink` redeems a `token_hash` through `verifyOtp`, an API call with the anon key that does not care what origin the page is on. Request the link from production, swap the host in the URL, and the session lands in localhost's storage. The permanent fix (allow-list localhost, swap `.SiteURL` for `.RedirectTo`) is still the right one, but the config that looks like a blocker frequently isn't one.

**Verified** by 12 unit tests — 9 on the extracted module (including 200 real dealt-and-scored seeds, the malformed-input guard, and escaping) and 3 pinning `bestRunDate` — and by driving all three surfaces in a real browser through import-map stubs, so the *real* `initLeaderboard` and `initProfile` render against realistic scored runs rather than a hand-built DOM. Screenshotted in light, dark and at 375px — which is the step that caught the discarded cards being too faint to read on the dark theme at the opacity that looked clearly-secondary on the light one. 579 unit tests pass.

---

### 11ac. Public Leaderboards and Profiles ✅ built (owner: "i want to make the leaderboard public and able to see without signing in, take into account clicking on profile and what the add friend button should do when not signed in")

A signed-out visitor now sees the boards, can click through to any player's profile, and can open the breakdown of any finished hand. Owner chose the widest of the three options offered, and "Add Friend stays visible and opens the login modal" over hiding or disabling it — it is the moment somebody most wants an account, which makes it the best signup prompt on the site.

**The obvious implementation publishes the whole table, and that is why it wasn't used.** Widening migration 006's policy from `to authenticated` to include `anon` would work and would also make `GET /rest/v1/daily_plays?select=*` a public endpoint against the anon key that ships in every page — returning every column of every finished row, including `seed`, and including every column that table ever gains afterwards. Nobody would have to remember to re-check that when adding one. So `daily_plays` stayed shut to `anon` and the reads were elevated instead: four `security definer` functions that each enumerate their own output columns, which *is* the public surface.

**This reverses §11j's choice of `security invoker`, for the reason §11j gave.** Its rule was to elevate only when a function must do what the caller cannot, and not when it reads exactly what the caller may already read. The second case was true while every caller was signed in and is false now — an anonymous caller may read none of this directly, which is precisely the condition named for elevating. The cost is real and worth stating: `security definer` bypasses RLS, so `result is not null` — the clause that stops anyone reading the seed of a hand its owner hasn't locked in yet (§11c) — now has to be repeated in each function body instead of being inherited from one policy.

**A pre-existing leak, found by asking what a signed-out visitor can already reach.** `profiles`' select policy has been `using (true)` with no role restriction since the first schema, so `?select=username,is_admin&is_admin=eq.true` has always named the admin to anyone who asked. That matters because §11f's threat model is "one compromised admin becomes JavaScript in every session", and knowing which account to attack is step one. Closed with a column-level grant rather than a policy change, because the rest of the row genuinely is public — a nameplate renders for anyone. Nothing broke, because nothing in the client reads that column from the table: `state/admin.js` calls the `is_admin()` *function* and the admin player list comes from `admin_overview()`, which is `security definer` and unaffected by grants. `authenticated` keeps its existing access deliberately — narrowing it too would need `getProfile`'s `select('*')` rewritten first, and leaving it alone means this migration cannot regress a signed-in path.

**Deploy order cannot break anything**, the rule §11z set. The client calls RPCs that do not exist until the SQL is run, so both new reads fall back to the query they replace — a signed-in player's profile views and hand breakdowns keep working in the window between pushing the bundle and running the migration. The one thing with no fallback is the boards for a signed-out visitor, since that is a new capability rather than a replaced one; it degrades to a plain "these boards aren't public yet, log in" rather than a raw Postgres error, which is exactly the state those visitors were already in.

**Two controls needed a decision rather than a default.** The Global/Friends toggle is *hidden* when signed out, not disabled: with no account there is no friends list for it to describe, so both of its states would show the same board — a control whose options are indistinguishable is worse than no control. Add Friend goes the other way and stays a live button, because there the click has somewhere useful to go. It asks the header for its login modal through a window event on `nameplate.js` rather than importing it, since that modal is a closure inside `initHeader` that owns the session, the username prompt and §11b's duplicate guard, none of which should get a second implementation. Same reasoning as §11e's cosmetic refresh.

**Verified** by driving both pages in a browser through import-map stubs whose `getSession` returns null — a genuine signed-out render of the real `initLeaderboard` and `initProfile`, not a mocked DOM. The assertion that matters most is the negative one: no sign-out, delete, rename or customise control appears anywhere on a profile viewed without a session, and `canEdit` is what guarantees it. Also checked that `?profile` with no username still refuses (there is no "your profile" without a you), that Add Friend dispatches the login request with the right hint, and that a breakdown modal still opens. The migration self-tests on execution (§11y — put the test where the code runs), asserting `anon` cannot read `is_admin`, can still read `username`, can execute all four functions, and that no policy on `daily_plays` names `anon`. 584 unit tests pass.

---

### 11ad. Per-Route Titles, Canonicals and Indexing ✅ built (owner: "i was looking at google seo tips and it said to have multiple different tab page titles, i want the titles to each page to be different like the profile, leaderboard, and normal page")

Every route now names itself: **Cardle — Daily Poker Puzzle**, **Leaderboards — Cardle**, **CAR — Cardle**, **Your Profile — Cardle**, **Admin — Cardle**.

**The title was the visible half; the canonical was the half that mattered.** `index.html` hardcoded `<link rel="canonical" href="https://cardle.lol/">` on every route, which tells Google that `?leaderboard` *is* the homepage — so a distinct title there would have been decoration on a page Google had been instructed not to index separately. `sitemap.xml` said the same thing in a different way, with exactly one entry. Both were correct when written, and **both rested on a premise that expired last commit**: their stated reason was that "the profile and leaderboard pages render nothing without a session, so a crawler sees an empty shell", which §11ac made false. Shipping only the titles would have looked done and changed nothing.

**What this actually buys, stated honestly.** The tab title works immediately and everywhere, because that is a browser reading `document.title`. The SEO half is weaker than it looks: the body is empty until JavaScript runs (§11w), so a crawler's *first* pass sees `index.html`'s static head on every route. Google executes JS and will pick these up on a second pass that can lag by days; Bing and most social unfurlers will not see them at all. Real per-route SEO needs real per-route HTML — separate static files behind Vercel rewrites, each with its own head — which duplicates `index.html` and touches the exact area that produced §11a's "unstyled page" deploy bug. Recorded as the available upgrade rather than done silently, because the difference between "the tab says the right thing" and "Google indexes it as its own page" is the whole reason the request was made.

**Profiles get titles but stay out of the index**, which is the one place the request and the SEO advice pull apart. More indexed pages is generally the goal; a page *per player* is not. There is one for every account and the set is unbounded, which is the thin-content pattern that drags a small site's whole quality signal down; they change every day, so anything indexed is stale within a day; and putting real players' usernames and statistics into search results is a decision about somebody other than the site owner. So they carry `noindex` plus a `Disallow`. The `noindex` is the half that binds — a `Disallow` only asks a crawler not to *fetch*, and a URL it never fetched can still be indexed from inbound links, which is the trap that makes robots.txt look like it failed.

**The `robots` tag is created and removed rather than toggled between values.** `noindex` and `index` are not opposites a crawler weighs against each other; absence *is* the indexable state. Building it on demand also means `index.html` ships without one, so no bug in this module can de-index the homepage — the failure worth designing against, since that is the page that actually earns traffic.

**Everything goes through DOM properties and `setAttribute`, never `innerHTML`**, so a username arriving from the URL cannot inject markup into `<head>`.

**Verified** by 10 unit tests on the pure `metaForRoute` (titles all distinct, each route canonicalising to itself rather than home, the homepage never noindexed, `?profile=` with a blank or whitespace name meaning *your* profile rather than a player called nothing, URL-significant characters encoded, and `?admin` winning over other params to match `main.js`'s routing order), plus a browser pass over all five routes reading the real `<head>` after render. 594 unit tests pass.

---

### 11ae. Google Ads Tag ✅ built (owner pasted the gtag.js snippet for account AW-18368131357)

The conversion/remarketing tag for the cardle.lol ad campaign, in `public/index.html`.

**Placed after `<meta charset>`, not literally first.** Google's instructions say "immediately after the `<head>` element". The charset declaration has to land inside the first 1024 bytes or a browser may guess an encoding and re-parse the document, and the snippet is ~350 bytes of that budget. The tag is `async`, so what matters is that the request *starts* early, not that it is byte zero — sitting second costs nothing and keeps the encoding declaration where the spec wants it. `document.characterSet` is asserted as UTF-8 either way.

**The second external dependency, after esm.sh's Supabase SDK**, and worth naming because the rule elsewhere has been to avoid them — §11v declined a branded remote image for the support link on exactly these grounds. Verified in a browser with the tag reachable AND with `googletagmanager.com` aborted: `gtag` is defined, no page errors, and the board renders and plays identically in both. Nothing waits on it.

**It will undercount, and that is the expected behaviour rather than a fault to chase.** googletagmanager.com is on every ad blocker's default list, so a real share of visits never report. §11w learned this when a blocker ate the Share button. The numbers are a floor and a trend; `daily_plays` is the headcount.

**Two things deliberately NOT done, recorded so they are decisions rather than oversights.** The tag gives page views and remarketing only — conversion tracking needs a conversion action defined in the Ads account plus a `gtag('event', 'conversion', …)` fired at a real moment (locking in a hand is the obvious primary). And the site has no consent banner: the campaign can serve in the EU, this tag sets cookies, and GDPR/ePrivacy plus Google's own EU user consent policy expect consent before that fires. Flagged to the owner rather than decided for them, because the options — a banner, excluding EU targeting, or accepting the risk — are a business call, not a technical one.

---

### 11af. Hand-Rankings Cheat Sheet and First-Visit Intro ✅ built (owner: "a lot of people dont know what any poker hands are so i would like to make a little cheat sheet for people to look at, it should be easy to access for new players, i sent it to a random person and he had trouble what the game was about")

Two problems in one report, and only one of them was the cheat sheet.

**The rules were already there and nobody was reading them.** How to Play has explained the game since §11a and was rewritten for accuracy in §11w — it is good, and it sits behind a `?` icon in the header that a first-timer does not click. So the fix is not better copy, it is *arrival*: the first visit ever now opens How to Play automatically, and a permanent "New here? How to play" link sits beside the cards for anyone who dismissed it or came back a week later. The owner chose both over either alone.

**Once, ever — and the flag is written BEFORE the modal opens**, not when it is dismissed. Somebody who closes the tab mid-read has still seen it; the alternative reopens on every visit until they happen to click the ×, which turns an introduction into something to get rid of. `localStorage` is wrapped because it throws outright in some privacy modes, and every branch fails toward "just play" — failing to show an intro must never stop the game loading.

**The cheat sheet is its own modal because it answers a question asked at a different moment.** How to Play is read once, before the first hand. "Does a Flush beat a Straight?" is asked mid-decision with three cards already marked — so it opens from a button next to the cards, not from a tutorial you would have to scroll past. How to Play links to it as well, for the player reading the rules cold, and that link is inside the modal so they do not have to close it and hunt.

**Two of the twelve ranks are not poker, and hiding that would be worse than not shipping a cheat sheet.** Three Straight and Four Straight are Cardle's own (§3w), and they land where a poker player would be actively misled: a Four Straight beats Two Pair here, a Three Straight beats a Pair. Both carry a `CARDLE ONLY` tag and the modal says so in prose. A reference that quietly reorders a game its readers think they know is a trap, not a help.

**One wording for "what is a Full House".** The descriptions come from `HAND_DESCRIPTIONS`, now exported from `score-breakdown.js` rather than rewritten — the same line teaches it here and reminds you of it on the score badge. Example hands are hand-picked rather than generated, because a randomly valid example often does not *read* as obviously that hand, which is the entire job.

**`openHelpModal` moved to module scope and is exported.** It was a closure inside `initHeader` and read no header state, so nothing kept it there but where it happened to be written — and the board needs it twice over now. That is a smaller change than the event-passing §11ac needed for the login modal, which genuinely does own session state.

**Pass 2 — the intro was still intimidating** (owner: "make the how to play a little wider and simplify the text, it looks very intimidating when it first opens up, also make the wild and rare card explanations start collapsed"). Auto-opening it raised the bar on the copy: this is now the first thing a brand-new player sees, and it was five headings and nine paragraphs of prose. The goal is one line, the steps went from five to four short ones, and Wilds, rare cards and the daily modifier collapsed into three `<details>` — phrased as the questions a player actually asks *while looking at the board* ("Why is that card glowing?") rather than as topic headings. The modal went to 540px in the same pass, because half of "intimidating" was measure: short copy still reads as a wall when every line wraps. **It now fits without scrolling at all**, which is the real change — the previous version was a scrollbar on open.

**Native `<details>`, not a scripted accordion.** Keyboard accessible and screen-reader-correct for free, works before any JS runs, and there is no open/closed state for the module to get out of step with the DOM. Its default triangle has to be suppressed twice (`list-style` for Firefox and modern Chrome, `::-webkit-details-marker` for older WebKit) — and the custom chevron first used `⌄`, which has no glyph in the default Windows UI font and fell back to something indistinguishable from a lowercase `v`. It read as a typo rather than a control. `▾` is a filled triangle that exists everywhere.

**Verified** in a browser across the full flow: a first-ever visit auto-opens How to Play; a second visit does not, and the stored flag proves why; the cheat sheet renders all 12 ranks in score order with five example cards each; exactly the two Cardle-only ranks carry the tag; the board button still works on a return visit; all three `<details>` start closed and open on click; and nothing overflows at 375px or 900px, with no hand name clipped at either. 594 unit tests pass.

---

### 4g. Even Money Was Called That on Odd Days ✅ fixed (owner: "the modifier 'even money' while the even/odd being odd, should change the modifier name to 'odd money', or just have 2 modifiers, odd money and even money")

The parity modifier is parameterised — `resolveModifier` rolls `ctx.parity` as `'even'` or `'odd'` per day — and `describe()` has always written the correct half. Only `label` was a hardcoded `'Even Money'`, so on roughly half of all parity days the banner named one rule and the sentence beneath it described the opposite one.

**Kept as one parameterised modifier rather than split into two, which was the owner's other option.** Two entries would double how often a parity day comes up in the rotation — a balance change nobody asked for, and invisible until someone noticed parity landing twice as often as Rainbow. Same reasoning §4f used for Hot Hand: one parameterised modifier beats twelve near-identical ones.

**`label` now resolves the way `description` always has.** An optional `resolveLabel(ctx)` on the definition, applied in `resolveModifier`, so the returned object still carries `label` as a plain string and every consumer — the board banner, the leaderboard's flipped-day note, the admin preview — reads it unchanged.

**The unresolved entry had to change too, and to something that commits to neither half.** `admin.js` and the test-mode picker list `MODIFIERS` directly, before any roll has happened, so a static `'Even Money'` there is a claim they cannot honour. It reads `Even / Odd Money` — which is what those pickers are actually offering.

**Verified** by 5 tests, all confirmed RED against the code they replace. The load-bearing one is a property rather than a case: *the label and the description never disagree*, checked across both halves — that is the defect stated directly, and it is what a future third parity variant would have to keep true. One more pins that every modifier WITHOUT `resolveLabel` keeps its static label, so a mistyped property name cannot silently blank one. 599 unit tests pass.

---

### 11ag. The Standing Chip Ranked the Wrong Run ✅ fixed (owner: "i had a run in the test panel that was billions of points and it said it was top 25%, also i want it out of everyone, not just the top 25")

**Two reports, and only one was a bug.**

**The field was already everyone.** `daily_standing` has no limit — `finished` selects every completed row for the game day and `total` counts all of them. "TOP 25%" is a percentage of the whole day's play, not "the top 25"; the leaderboard's `BOARD_SIZE = 25` is a different function that shares nothing with this one. Worth recording that the wording invites the misreading, because the chip deliberately hides the denominator (§11aa: the owner asked for no "of xxx today") and the tooltip is the only place that says otherwise.

**The chip was accurate about a run nobody was looking at.** Migration 016 ranks whatever sits in the caller's own `daily_plays` row: `mine as (select score from finished where user_id = auth.uid())`. Test mode never persists (`persist: false`, `state/test-mode.js`), so a test run is not in that table — the chip was faithfully reporting where the player's REAL run placed, printed underneath a test score it had never seen. That is worse than a visibly broken number, because there is nothing to notice: a plausible percentage under the wrong hand.

**The code said so out loud and nobody read it.** `renderStanding(resultPanel, total)` has taken a `total` argument since §11aa and never passed it anywhere — `fetchDailyStanding()` was called with no arguments at all. **A parameter that is accepted and ignored looks exactly like a parameter that works**, at the call site and in every review since. It is now threaded through to an optional `for_score`, so the chip ranks the run it sits under.

**The compatibility shim broke the thing it was protecting** — caught by probing the deployed endpoint, and worth recording in full. 018 added the two-argument form and deliberately kept the one-argument one, reasoning that Postgres resolves overloads by arity so the already-deployed bundle would keep working. Postgres does. **PostgREST does not**: it matches an RPC on the JSON keys in the request body, so `{"day": null}` is a valid call to *both* signatures — the second defaults `for_score` — and it returns `PGRST203 Could not choose the best candidate function` rather than picking. The live site's standing chip stopped appearing the moment 018 ran, silently, because `fetchDailyStanding` resolves null on any error by design (§11aa). Migration 019 drops the one-argument form; with a single candidate PostgREST resolves both call shapes against it, so the old bundle and the new one both work from one function. The overload was never buying anything.

**018's own self-test passed**, which is exactly why it was missed: it calls the function from inside SQL, where Postgres's arity resolution is the one that applies. Only an HTTP call through PostgREST can see this class of fault — §11y's rule, re-learned.

**The client also retries without the score** if the two-argument form is missing, so the SQL and the bundle can be deployed in either order (§11z's rule) — verified by stubbing the new signature to fail and confirming the second call lands and the chip still renders. That path now only covers "018 was never run at all"; while both overloads existed, both calls failed.

**Ranking an arbitrary number is a deliberate, small widening.** A signed-in caller can now pass a score and learn how many people beat it. The boards already publish the top 25 of every window, `total` was already returned, and nothing here exposes another player's identity or hand. Still `security invoker`, still `authenticated` only.

**Verified** by driving `fetchDailyStanding` through a stubbed client and asserting what actually reaches the wire — `for_score: 9123456789` on the happy path, and a two-call fallback ending in a rendered chip when the new signature is rejected. That is the assertion the original lacked: not "does a chip appear" but "does the score the player is looking at reach the thing that ranks it". The migration self-tests on execution, checking that an unbeatable score ranks 1st and a last-place score ranks `field + 1`.

### 11ai. Friend-Request Notification Badge ✅ built (owner: "i want to add a notification on the friends buton if youve received a friend invite")

**A count, not a dot.** The panel behind the button already shows counts, and "3" answers the next question ("is it worth opening?") without a click. Capped at `9+` so the pill stays a circle — past nine, the exact number is not what makes someone look. The accessible name keeps the real figure: the badge is `aria-hidden` and `friendsBtn` carries `aria-label="Friends, 15 pending friend requests"`, so a screen reader gets the truth rather than the truncation, and hears the count once rather than twice.

**A cheap query, not the existing one.** `getPendingRequests()` was already there and returns exactly what is needed if you take `.length` — but it fetches every pending row and then makes a SECOND round trip through `profilesById()` to attach usernames, purely so the panel can render nameplates. For a number on a button that is two rowful queries where none are needed. `getPendingRequestCount()` uses PostgREST's `head: true` with `count: 'exact'`, which returns the count and no rows at all.

**THE HAMBURGER NEEDED ITS OWN MARK, and this is the part that would have shipped broken.** Under the 560px breakpoint the entire nav — Friends button included — collapses behind ☰ (§11t). A badge only on the button is therefore invisible on phones, which is where a daily game is mostly played: the notification would have appeared exactly where nobody could see it, and looked completely correct in every desktop screenshot. The ☰ gets a dot instead of the number, because it is a small glyph and a digit on it reads as clutter; its job is only "there is something in here", which the count inside then answers. The rule is declared INSIDE the media query rather than overridden outside it, so above the breakpoint — where the real count is visible — the dot does not exist at all rather than being hidden.

**Refreshed at three moments, none of them a timer.** On `refreshProfile()` (so, every session resolution and auth change, including sign-out clearing a stale badge); on the friends panel CLOSING rather than after each accept/decline, which is one query for a whole session of tidying up and cannot leave a stale "2" behind a panel now showing none; and on `visibilitychange` back to visible, which costs nothing while the tab is hidden and is the moment someone is most likely to be looking at the header. No polling: a daily game does not earn a heartbeat.

**Fails closed.** A blocked CDN, an offline player or an RLS surprise leaves the count at 0 and the header looks exactly as it did before this existed. `getPendingRequestCount` itself throws like every other function in friends.js — header.js is what decides a failure means "no badge", and it can only make that call if the failure actually reaches it, so the not-configured test asserts the throw rather than a 0.

**The colour took three measurements and the first two were wrong.** `var(--red)` is the obvious choice: it exists, it is theme-aware, it is red. It is also a FOREGROUND token — the pips on a red card — so its dark value (#e0665c) is lightened to sit on a dark background, and used as a FILL under white text it measures **3.37:1**, under the 4.5:1 AA needs at 10.88px. Replaced with one fixed colour, since a notification is a fixed signal rather than something that should restyle itself. The first fixed pick, #c0392b, measured 5.44:1 on white text and 3.35:1 against the background — **except that figure was taken against the PAGE background, and the badge does not sit on the page background.** It sits on `#site-header`'s `--surface`, where the same colour is **2.96:1** and fails the 3:1 non-text minimum. The value is boxed in from both sides — darker helps the text and sinks the pill, lighter does the reverse — and #cf4436 clears both: white text 4.63:1, pill against the header 3.47:1 dark and 4.63:1 light.

**Verified** in a real browser at 900px and 375px, both themes: counts 0/1/2/9/15 render `""`/`1`/`2`/`9`/`9+` with correct singular/plural in the accessible name; the pill is a circle (18.39px square, widening to 22.89px only for `9+`); with the menu open on mobile the badge is laid out inside the dropdown and stays within both the button and the nav; the ☰ dot exists below the breakpoint and not above it; no horizontal overflow at either width. 636 unit tests pass.

### 11aj. Full Day Reset ✅ built (owner: "i would like to add a full day reset button in the admin page")

**The remedy that only worked for one person.** `admin_reset_day(target_id, day)` has existed since §11f, and it clears ONE player's claimed hand. When something is wrong with the day itself — a bad modifier pin, a scoring bug that lands mid-day, a deal nobody should have to keep — the only available fix was to search each affected player and reset them one at a time. That stops being a remedy the moment more than a handful of people have played. `admin_reset_today_for_everyone()` clears the day in one statement.

**Today only, and the restriction lives in the database.** Resetting a PAST day is not a smaller version of the same action: XP, levels, streaks and achievements are all *derived* from `daily_plays` rows (§11d), so they move the instant the rows do, and the weekly and all-time boards rewrite themselves silently. Today is the only day where "reset" means "play it again"; for anything older, the per-player reset at least scopes the damage to the one person who asked. So `game_today()` is computed inside the function and there is no parameter that selects a day. Putting the date in the signature would have put the rule in the bundle — and **the bundle is public JavaScript, which has never been the boundary here** (§11f). A patched page can call any RPC with any argument; it cannot call a function that has nowhere to put the argument.

**`expected_day` is a guard, not a selector, and that distinction is the design.** It can only ever make the function REFUSE — passing last Tuesday aborts rather than deleting last Tuesday. It exists for one race that is otherwise completely silent: the page computes the day when it draws the confirmation, the server computes it when the delete runs, and at 19:00 New York those two instants straddle a rollover. An admin could read `2026-08-06`, type `2026-08-06` to confirm, and have the server clear `2026-08-07` — button works, row count comes back, notice names the day they were looking at. **Nothing about that is visible.** Sending the confirmed day back turns it into an error. Seconds-wide, but §11ag was seconds-wide too, and it cost five lines.

**The count in front of the delete had to be a different number than the one already on the page.** `admin_overview.runs_today` has always meant `result is not null` — *finished* runs. That is right for "how much play happened today" and wrong in front of a delete, because the reset also removes rows for people who are part way through a hand right now. Promising to clear 47 and clearing 51 is §11ag exactly: a plausible figure sitting under the wrong thing. `claimed_today` is the honest one, added to the existing `json` return so the page still makes one round trip and an older bundle simply ignores the extra key. When the server predates 020 the sentence is **omitted rather than guessed** — no number beats a wrong number.

**Deletes rather than blanks**, for the same reason 004 gives for the per-player version: a row with a null `result` means "seed claimed, not finished", so blanking the score would leave every player stuck on the hand they were already unhappy with. Deleting releases the seed and the next claim rolls a fresh one.

**Type-to-confirm, and the phrase is the date.** Same shape as Delete Player, which is the established pattern here for anything unrecoverable — and this is the only other button on the page that qualifies. A fixed word like RESET becomes muscle memory on the second use; the date forces a re-read of the one fact that actually goes wrong, and it is exactly the fact a tab left open across the rollover gets wrong. No `inputmode="numeric"` on that input, deliberately: the phrase contains hyphens and iOS's numeric keypad has no hyphen key, which would have made the confirmation untypeable on a phone.

**Rendered last, below even the per-player panel.** Everything above it acts on one account someone had to go looking for; this acts on everybody at once, and it should never be what a click lands on while heading somewhere else. It gets a red section border and red title rather than only a red button, because the button is the same `.profile-delete-btn` used to delete a single player and nothing else would say "this one is different". Border and title only — a filled panel reads as an error state on a page that already uses a red fill for precisely that (`.admin-notice--error`).

**Two things it cannot reach, and the panel says so.** A signed-out player's result lives in their own browser's `localStorage` (§11c), which no server-side delete can touch, so they will still be told they've played today. And anyone with the game already open keeps their finished board until they reload. Both are unfixable from here; leaving them unstated would make the reset look broken rather than bounded.

**Not routed through `withNotice`.** That helper re-reads the *selected player*, whose panel would then be describing a day that no longer exists. The reset drops the selection and reloads the overview instead, which is what is actually true afterwards.

**The self-test is deliberately not behavioural, which is worth saying out loud rather than leaving to look like an oversight.** Every other migration's self-test can exercise the real thing because reading is free. The only way to prove this function deletes is to let it delete every run of the live day — a self-test that destroys the data it checks is not a test, it is the incident. So 020 asserts the properties whose failure would be *silent*: exactly one signature (019's PGRST203), `security definer` — as `security invoker` RLS would quietly delete only the admin's own row and report "1 hand cleared", which looks like a working button — `anon` denied execute, exactly one argument so nobody has since turned the guard into a selector, and `claimed_today` present in `admin_overview`'s stored definition. That last one is read from `pg_get_functiondef` rather than by CALLING the function, because **the SQL Editor runs with no JWT**: `auth.uid()` is null, `is_admin()` is false, and the call would raise for the very admin applying the migration.

**Verified.** 638 unit tests pass (`tests/admin.test.js` is new — `src/state/admin.js` had no coverage at all before this).
