Wire-level assertions against a stubbed client, run separately because they need `--experimental-test-module-mocks` and the project's declared minimum is Node 20, which does not have it: the call reaches `admin_reset_today_for_everyone` with a body of exactly `{expected_day}` and nothing else; the row count is returned as a number and a null/garbage shape reports 0 rather than `NaN`; a `PGRST202` names `020-full-day-reset.sql` instead of surfacing "Could not find the function", which reads like an outage; every other error passes through untouched. **Still to do on localhost: run migration 020, then confirm the section renders with a live `claimed_today`, that the typed date arms the button, and that the notice reports a real row count.**

### 11ak. The Run That Was Lost By Looking At The Leaderboard ✅ fixed (owner bug report, second-hand: "my friend said he got a flush when he drew his cards, but while it was adding up the points he clicked on the leaderboards and it never showed his name on the leaderboards, it reset his whole hand and he got a much worse hand and had no idea what happened. i tried to recreate it and could not")

**Reproduced exactly, in a browser, before anything was changed.** Lock in a hand, wait for the submission to be in flight, click Leaderboards, come back: the board offers the hand again. Everything the report describes follows from that one fact, and the reason it would not reproduce by hand is that reproducing it requires clicking away *during* the window — which is only wide enough to notice if the request is slow.

**The result had exactly one copy, and it was in flight.** `lockIn` fires `saveTodayResultForUser` un-awaited and immediately starts `revealScore`. That is the right call for the reveal — the count-up should not wait on an Edge Function that may be cold-starting — but it leaves a finished run existing nowhere except an open request. **The header sits above that animation the entire time and every button in it is a full page navigation** (there is no router, §11d), and a navigation cancels an in-flight fetch. Ten seconds of counting up, with a clickable Leaderboards button, is not an edge case; it is an invitation.

**Nothing was written locally, because the local write is in the `else`.** `saveTodayResult` — the localStorage path — runs only for signed-OUT players. The signed-in branch had no on-disk copy at all, so when the request died there was nothing left of the run anywhere. Now the run is mirrored to localStorage FIRST, synchronously, before the network is touched, and the next load resubmits it. The submission stays un-awaited; the mirror is what makes that safe rather than lucky.

**`result: null` means two different things and the code only knew one.** A claimed row with no result means "seed claimed, not finished" — which is why §11aj's `admin_reset_day` deletes rather than blanks. It ALSO means "the submission was cancelled before it landed", and from the board those are indistinguishable. A pending mirror for the same seed is what separates them.

**Resubmitting is gated on the seed matching, and that gate is the whole safety argument.** Resending a run computed from the deal the server is still holding is the identical request that was already in flight, not a second attempt at the day. A mirror from any other seed — an admin day reset re-rolls it, a new game day claims a fresh row — is discarded. `pendingRunMatches` is pure and exported so that one dangerous line is unit-tested rather than left to a browser test; the storage it reads is localStorage-bound and its caller is a DOM module, which between them would have covered it nowhere. It compares as NUMBERS, because the seed round-trips through JSON on one side and a Postgres `bigint` on the other and PostgREST may hand that back as a string — a strict `!==` fails closed and silently turns the whole feature into a no-op.

**A 409 is what a successful retry looks like, and treating it as fatal is what made this unrecoverable.** `submit-run` answers 409 for both "no hand claimed today" and "today is already finished", and `isFunctionMissing` let neither through — so a resubmission of a run that *had* landed (only its response was lost) died as a rejected cheat. It now reads the row back: a stored result means the run is in, and it is the server's result, which is the one to display anyway (§11z). Nothing is trusted from the client to reach that conclusion; it is a read.

**THE HARSHER VARIANT, WHICH IS THE ONE THAT PRODUCES A DIFFERENT HAND.** A replayed null-result row deals the SAME five cards, so it alone cannot explain "a much worse hand" unless the player discarded differently the second time. `beginRealPlay` had a second failure mode that can: `getSession()` collapses "the backend says nobody is signed in" and "we could not reach the backend to ask" into one `null`, and on that null the board fell through to `getOrCreateTodaySeed()` — **a brand-new local seed, a completely unrelated deal, and no message of any kind**, while the account hand sat on the server untouched. The same connectivity blip that cancels a POST is a plausible cause of both halves of the report. `resolveSession()` now returns a third state, `unavailable`; a browser that has resolved a real session before is told we could not check and offered Try again, rather than being quietly handed someone else's day. Same shape and same lesson as §11i's login flash: **an unknown value must not default to one of the two known ones.**

**Never deal when dealing would mean dealing the WRONG hand.** The claim-failed branch fell through to the same fresh local seed. We know who the player is and we know their hand exists; the only honest move is to say we could not fetch it. The fallback existed so a Supabase hiccup could not block the game entirely — a good instinct that had the wrong remedy attached, because "keep playing" was implemented as "play something else".

**The recovery is bounded, and finding out why is what the browser test was for.** The first version awaited the resubmission with no timeout, and a hung request left the page on "Loading today's hand…" indefinitely — a worse failure than the one being fixed, introduced by the fix. It now races an 8s timeout, and on expiry deliberately KEEPS the mirror and falls through to the hand the player was going to be offered anyway. A test that only asserted the happy path would have shipped this.

**A third symptom was real and separate: the leaderboard told him the wrong thing.** When the SDK cannot load, `userId` is null for a signed-in player, and the error copy read that as signed-out and said *"These boards aren't public yet. Log in to see how you stack up."* That is the sentence a player meets when they go looking for a score that is missing — wrong about the problem, at the exact moment they are trying to understand it. `sessionUnknown` is checked first now.

**Storage that cannot be written is the one variant needing no network fault at all**, and it reproduces the entire report on its own: with localStorage blocked (Safari private mode, "block all cookies", a full quota) `getOrCreateTodaySeed` can never keep the seed it mints, so **every single load deals a new hand** and no result is ever remembered. Every helper in persistence.js degrades quietly on a throw — individually right, collectively a game that silently re-deals forever. Probed once now, and said out loud.

**Verified.** 644 unit tests (6 new, all confirmed RED against both the inline check they replace and the first version written here — the strict compare rejects a string seed, the `Number()` compare matches two absent seeds to each other). Plus four browser scenarios against a stubbed backend, the first of which is the bug report performed literally: mirror written before the POST resolves, run accepted on the way back, hand NOT re-offered, result panel shown, mirror cleared. Run against the pre-fix code the same scenario reports `the board does NOT offer the hand again: got true` — the defect, stated directly. Scenarios 3 and 4 pin that an unresolvable session no longer deals a local hand to someone with an account, and that a genuinely signed-out player with a blocked CDN still plays exactly as before.

**Still to do on localhost: play a real hand signed in, click Leaderboards mid-count-up, and come back** — the stub proves the logic, not the live Edge Function's timing.

### 11al. Stack the Deck ✅ built (owner: "make a thing where i can set someones cards for the next reset they do, i want to give them the exact hand that draws and if they discard some, i can pick what they get from that discard")

**The feature is easy; making the server agree is the feature.** §11z made scoring authoritative — `submit-run` re-deals from the seed and recomputes the total, ignoring whatever the client reports. So a rigged deal that existed only in the browser would be re-scored from the ORDINARY deal: the player would watch a Royal Flush land and then be paid for five cards nobody ever saw. The stacked cards therefore had to become server-held state, read by both halves and turned into a deal by one shared pure function (`dealFromStack`, `core/deck.js`) — the §11z purity boundary paying out a fourth time.

**Determinism is the whole contract, and it is the thing that would only break in production.** The board and the Edge Function are different machines; they agree only because `dealFromStack` derives every un-pinned card from the row's own seed. A `freshSeed()` in there — which is exactly what the test-mode Custom Hand Builder uses, legitimately, since nothing verifies it — would pass every local test and reject every real rigged run. That is a property test, not a case: *the same seed and stack always build the identical deal.*

**NOT A COLUMN ON `daily_plays`, and this is the part that would have quietly leaked.** Migration 006 added `using (result is not null)` as a TABLE-level select policy, so every signed-in player can read every column of every finished run. A `stacked_deal` column there would have published *which runs were rigged* to anyone who opened devtools — §11ac's lesson exactly ("opening a feature is not the same as opening a table", and "every column added to it in future" comes along for free). It lives in its own table whose select policy is own-rows-only, so the player is dealt their cards and nobody else learns anything.

**One table, two states, and the difference is `play_date`:** null means QUEUED for their next hand, set means ATTACHED to that day. §11j's warning about a table holding two kinds of row applies, which is why the state is a single nullable column rather than a status string — there is no third value to get wrong — with a partial unique index making each state's rule explicit (at most one queued deal per player; at most one attached per player per day).

**A TRIGGER ATTACHES IT, NOT THE CLIENT.** The claim is a plain browser INSERT into `daily_plays`, and a browser cannot be trusted to consume its own rigging — a patched page would simply skip the step. An `after insert` trigger moves the queued row onto the day being claimed, inside the same statement that claims the seed, so the two can never disagree about which day the cards belong to. `security definer`, because the queued row is unreachable to the inserting player: they have select on their own rows and no write privilege at all.

**Queued deals are replaceable; attached ones are frozen.** Rewriting cards that a player may be looking at right now would leave their board and the server's scorer building different deals — the one failure this design exists to prevent. So `admin_queue_stacked_deal` replaces only the pending row, and `admin_clear_stacked_deal` can only cancel a pending one.

**Validated three times, on purpose, and refusal beats repair.** `normalizeStackedDeal` is the single rule, run by the admin page (so a duplicate card is refused with a sentence naming the slot), by the board, and by `verifyAndScoreRun`. The database repeats the structural floor because §11x's rule stands — a validation rule the client owns is not a rule — and §11y's stands too: admin-authored content is untrusted content, since this JSON reaches a scorer on the server and a renderer in someone else's browser. Five cards exactly, because `evaluateHand` throws on anything else and it is called from the render path; no card twice, because one physical deck cannot deal the same card to the hand and the draw pile; an unknown rarity tier is REFUSED rather than dropped to null, since silently downgrading a card the admin deliberately made Diamond would change the score without saying so.

**The two halves fail the same way, which is what makes the fallback safe.** A malformed stack makes the board deal normally and makes the server refuse the run — different responses, same premise, so they never score different cards. The board's fallback is deliberate: a bad row should cost the surprise, not the player's day.

**No extra latency on the path everyone takes.** The stacked deal is fetched in PARALLEL with the claim, so a reload — the common case, for a feature that is almost never active — costs nothing. Only the first claim of the day has to look twice, because the trigger attaches the deal during that very insert and it cannot exist a moment earlier. The read resolves null on any failure, including the table not existing yet, so an unapplied migration 021 degrades to ordinary play rather than an unplayable board.

**It counts on the leaderboards, and the record of that lives in exactly one place** (owner's choice of three). The run is indistinguishable from a real one everywhere a player can see; the admin panel shows "⚑ Already dealt from a stack" for the days it applied. Recording it somewhere was the point — an untraceable version would leave no way to answer "was that real?" later.

**DEPLOY ORDER IS THE ONE THING THE MIGRATION CANNOT ENFORCE.** Run 021, deploy the updated `submit-run`, *then* queue a deal. A deal queued while the old function is live is scored from the ordinary deal. Stated in the migration's own success notice rather than only here, because that is what an admin actually reads.

**Verified.** 662 unit tests (18 new): the pinned cards are what is dealt, the deck is still 52 cards with nothing duplicated into the filler, rarity and wild survive onto pinned cards, a malformed stack is refused rather than repaired, the server scores the stacked cards and not the seed-dealt ones, a discard is served from the pinned pile in order, and — the regression that matters most — *no stacked deal leaves the ordinary deal byte-identical to before this existed*. Plus a browser run against a stubbed backend: A♠ K♠ Q♠ J♠ 2♣ dealt exactly, the pinned 10♠ (Diamond) arriving in the discarded slot, and the request body carrying `discardRounds` and no card or seed data at all.

**Two things the browser test caught that no assertion had been written for.** The deal animation leaves cards face-up but still DISABLED for a beat, and Playwright's `nth(-1)` is the last element rather than nothing — so a `findIndex` that returned -1 silently clicked the wrong card and reported a plausible failure about the wrong thing. And the day's real modifier applies to a stacked deal like any other: Locked Card locked the pinned 2♣, which is correct, and is worth knowing before wondering why a rigged hand did not play out as drawn.

**Reworked once, immediately, because the first shape answered the wrong question** (owner: "picking what the outcome is when they discard a card is not working like i wanted it to... instead of me listing the draw pile as in the order they get discarded, i pick the third slot as what i want it because they will discard the third slot"). The original `draws` was an ORDERED pile: the first pinned card went to whoever was discarded first. That only lands the card you meant if you can predict how many cards the player throws and in what order — the one thing an admin cannot know. `slotDraws` is a sparse array indexed by HAND SLOT: `slotDraws[2]` is dealt if and only if slot 2 is the card thrown away. Handing someone a straight from 2 3 Q 5 6 is now "put the 4 opposite the Q", which is how the request was phrased in the first place.

**The old shape is retired rather than reinterpreted.** An ordered pile and a slot map are both "an array of cards", so a 021-era row parses cleanly under the new reader and deals real cards to the WRONG SLOTS — plausible, silent and completely wrong. `normalizeStackedDeal` ignores `draws` entirely and migration 023 deletes any queued row that predates `slotDraws`. Attached rows are left alone: they belong to a day already in play, and removing one would leave the board and the server building different deals.

**FOUR COPIES OF "WHICH CARD LANDS WHERE" HAD TO BECOME ONE.** The replacement step existed twice in board.js (the live discard, the already-played re-render) and twice in verify-run.js (mid-round, final round). Per-slot pinning had to change all four identically or the player would be shown one hand and paid for another — §11y's rule, arriving with teeth. `applyDiscards` in core/deck.js is now the only definition, and *without* `slotDraws` it is byte-identical to what it replaced: both callers already sorted their indices ascending, and it consumes the pile in ascending slot order, so every ordinary deal maps exactly as before. That equivalence is a test, not an assertion in a comment.

**A pinned replacement is dealt at most once.** Second Look discards twice; a slot thrown away in both rounds takes its pinned card in round one and an ordinary one in round two. The same card arriving twice is something no physical deck can do.

**The rework exposed a real bug in the original: the two halves did NOT fail the same way.** §11al above claimed they did. In fact board.js fell back to an ordinary deal on a malformed stack while `verifyAndScoreRun` REFUSED the run — so a bad admin row meant the player played a completely normal hand and then had it rejected, losing the day to something they never saw and could not have caused. Both halves now ignore an unreadable stack, which is the only pairing where they agree *and* nobody is punished. The original comment was right that they could never score different cards, and wrong that this was sufficient.

**Verified.** 671 unit tests (27 in this file, up from 18). The headline case is the owner's own: 2 3 Q 5 6 with a 4 pinned opposite the Q, discard the Q, score a Straight — plus its negative, that discarding any *other* slot never hands over that 4. The load-bearing regression is `applyDiscards` with no pins consuming the pile in slot order, because that path is every player, every day. In the browser, a distinct replacement is pinned to all five slots and the assertion is that the discarded slot receives ITS card and no other slot's card appears — a test the ordered-pile model fails by construction. The admin panel's queued payload is asserted at the wire: `slotDraws: [null, null, {4♦}, null, null]`, holes preserved, no legacy `draws` key.

**The panel is one row per slot, and the layout took two passes.** Eight controls plus an arrow do not fit on one line at any realistic admin width, and the first version left the `→` stranded at the end of the wrapped first line, pointing at nothing. The arrow now LEADS the replacement group so it starts the second line, each slot gets a separating rule, and the replacement is indented so it reads as belonging to the card above rather than as a sixth slot.

**Still to do on localhost: run migration 023 and REDEPLOY `submit-run`** — `verify-run.js` and `deck.js` both changed, and the server half of a deal is only as current as the last deploy. ✅ done, alongside §11am.

### 11am. The Player Was Choosing Their Own Hand ✅ fixed (owner: "is there any possibility that this system can be rigged or manipulated through any way that a person can give themselves a royal flush" — then, with a screenshot of the table: "my friend has his seeds set for the future to all roll royal flushes")

**Exploited in production, found by asking the question rather than by a bug report.** Two accounts had pre-claimed every day from 2026-08-28 to 2026-09-04 with seed `2316669`, which deals K♦ 10♦ Q♦ J♦ A♦ — a pat Royal Flush, bronze rarity on the K for an extra multiplier. One of them had the current day claimed with it and unplayed. Nothing had banked yet: the top score in the table was 6,081,195 against a Royal's 54,800,000, and neither account appeared in the top fifteen. The window between setup and payout is what saved it, and that was luck.

**TWO HOLES, AND THEY ARE NOT PEERS.** The seed was supplied by the browser's INSERT and the policy constrained only *who* was inserting, never *what* — that is the vulnerability. `play_date` being unbounded is the amplifier: it turned one offline grind into a year of rigged days in a single statement. The owner's first read was that the second one was the bug, which is the natural reading of the screenshot, because the repeated seed across nine dates is what jumps out. Closing only that leaves the exploit fully working at one INSERT per night, from a client that simply never opens the page before 7pm.

**`dealHand` being pure is a design asset and an attack surface, and it was only ever documented as the first.** Ranks, suits, rarity tiers and wild flags all fall out of one 32-bit number, so the entire seed space is searchable offline in seconds using the game's own shipped module. Every property that makes the server able to re-derive an honest run makes an attacker able to pre-select a dishonest one. The two are the same property.

**THE COMMENT ASSERTING SAFETY IS WHY NOBODY LOOKED.** `verify-run.js` opened by calling the seed "the one thing the player cannot forge", and `schema.sql` carried seven lines explaining why `seed` must be write-once — with column grants that genuinely deliver write-once. **Write-once was the wrong property.** It stops a reroll; the first write is the only one an attacker wants. §11y already recorded that a comment saying "nothing here is untrusted" is a place to look rather than a reason not to, and this is the same failure one level up: the comment was not merely stale, it was load-bearing for a conclusion it never established.

**Overwrite, do not validate.** The trigger replaces `new.seed` with a CSPRNG value rather than rejecting a suspicious one, so there is no request shape that gets a chosen value through and no client change is required — `daily-play.js` still sends a seed, and it is discarded. A column `DEFAULT` was added as well, so dropping the trigger later degrades to server-minted rather than to client-chosen.

**`gen_random_uuid()` over `random()`**, masked to 32 bits. Postgres's `random()` is a per-backend seeded PRNG and in principle observable; a v4 UUID's first 32 bits are CSPRNG-backed. Masking to `[0, 2^32)` matches `freshSeed()`'s range, which matters because `createRng` is mulberry32 over `seed >>> 0` — a wider value would silently collapse onto the same state space anyway.

**PRIVILEGED CALLERS ARE EXEMPT, and skipping that would have corrupted real rows.** PostgREST sets the role per request, so `current_user` distinguishes a browser from the Edge Function, the SQL editor and the admin RPCs. Without the exemption, migration 022's self-test — which inserts `play_date '1999-01-01'` and cleans up by that date — would have written a real row for *today* against a real account and then failed to find it again.

**A ±1 DAY WINDOW, NOT A HARD PIN, AND REWRITING THE DATE WOULD HAVE BEEN A BUG.** The client reads its row back with `.eq('play_date', <its own gameDayFor()>)`, so silently storing a different date than the one asked for means the next load finds nothing, claims again, collides on the primary key, and serves §11ak's "we could not fetch your hand" screen. Rejecting is loud; rewriting is silent and wrong. The window is a day wide because client and server compute the game day from two different clocks and disagree for seconds either side of the 19:00 boundary — the busiest moment of the day — and an exact match would convert ordinary clock skew into a lockout precisely then. One day of slack is harmless *only because* the seed is now server-minted: pre-claiming ever had value solely when paired with a chosen seed.

**The cleanup runs itself, deliberately breaking 015's convention, because 015 is the reason this was survivable.** Migration 015 left its two `revoke` lines commented out as a considered second step. They *were* eventually run — confirmed against `information_schema.column_privileges`, which is the only way to know, since the repo records the intent and not the act. But the same pattern is what would have left an arbitrary-score hole open beside this one, and a step that exists only in a comment is a step that does not happen. Deleting an *unplayed* row is exactly what `admin_reset_day` already does, so it is safe to automate; the migration reports its counts rather than asking.

**A structural self-test would have passed against the broken version**, §11al's lesson arriving again. The only assertion worth making is that a client-shaped INSERT does not get the seed it asked for, so the self-test calls `set local role authenticated`, sets the `request.jwt.claims` that `auth.uid()` reads, and attempts the real exploit with the real seed. Without *both* of those it would pass for the wrong reason — the insert policy would refuse it before the trigger was ever reached.

**A latent ordering weakness in the cleanup, stated rather than hidden.** Future-dated rows are deleted first, then duplicate seeds. Had the first pass left only a *single* row carrying the rigged seed, the second would have seen `count(*) = 1` and spared it. It worked here because the game day was 08-27 and the `> game_today() + 1` filter left three rows sharing the seed — which is a fact about the calendar, not about the query.

**`verified` is not a provenance signal, and it looks exactly like one.** The flag lives *inside* the `result` jsonb, which is the same blob a client with UPDATE privilege would be writing. Its presence proves only that whoever wrote the row did not think to include it. A real marker has to live in a column the client cannot write.

**A second, unrelated bug fell out of the same audit: Double or Nothing was never resolved server-side.** `modifierScoringMultiplier` reads `dailyModifier.wagered`, which `resolveModifier()` never sets — board.js stamps it in place at selection time. `verify-run.js` was calling it with the bare resolved modifier, so the server's multiplier was **1 on every wager day**: a busted wager kept its full undoubled score while the screen showed a wipe, and a won wager silently lost its ×2. `wagered` was already threaded into `verifyAndScoreRun` and stopped at the discard limits. This was mis-scoring honest players in both directions and had nothing to do with cheating.

**The first version of that test asserted the wrong rule and the code was right.** A busted wager was expected to score 0; it scores 150, because Pity Points (§3i) are added *after* the modifier multiplier. Asserting zero would have pinned a rule the game does not have. The assertion is now `total === pity` — everything except the floor is wiped — which states the actual behaviour and survives a retune of the pity award.

**Verified.** 674 unit tests (3 new), all confirmed RED against the pre-fix `modifierScoringMultiplier(modifier)` call — reverted and re-run, not assumed, per §11q. Both directions of the wager are asserted separately, because a fix that stamps the field but reads the wrong side of the threshold would still pass one of them. The seed half is verified in SQL rather than Node: it is a database trigger, and no unit test can observe the role PostgREST switches to.

### 11an. Comments Off The Wire, Not Out Of The Repo ✅ built (owner: "i would like to remove all comments made on the site so nobody can right click and inspect and just see all the comments you made, it looks trashy")

**The request had two readings and only one of them is survivable.** Deleting the comments from `src/` would have answered it literally and destroyed the most valuable thing in the project — §11am exists *because* a wrong comment went unexamined for months, and `verify-run.js`'s header is now the only record that a database trigger is what holds §11z up. The rule is comments in the repo, none on the wire, which means a build step rather than an edit.

**This was a no-build project, and that was the whole reason the comments were visible.** `vercel.json` sets `outputDirectory: "."` and the browser imports `/src/**/*.js` directly, so the source IS the bundle: 5,484 comment lines, including DESIGN.md section references and "ACCURACY NOTE: do not write X here", were one devtools click away. 542 KB now comes off the download.

**Checked the actual exposure first rather than assuming it.** `.vercelignore` already existed and was doing real work — `DESIGN.md`, `supabase/`, `tests/` and `tools/` all return 404 on production, probed rather than trusted. So the scope really was just `src/`, which is the one directory that is public *by design*.

**MINIFY IN PLACE, WHICH IS ONLY SAFE BECAUSE OF WHERE IT RUNS.** Vercel builds from an ephemeral checkout, so rewriting files there costs nothing and means zero path changes: `index.html` still points at `/src/ui/main.js`, the dev server still serves commented source, and there is no `dist/` whose contents could drift from `src/`. The alternative — build to `dist/` and rewrite `/src/*` — does not work here anyway, because Vercel checks the filesystem before rewrites and the real file would win. The cost is a script that is destructive against a working tree, so it refuses to run without `VERCEL=1` or an explicit `--force`.

**`transform`, not `bundle`.** Per-file minification leaves the module graph exactly as it is — 52 modules still fetched as 52 modules, import specifiers untouched, and `supabase-client.js`'s dynamic `import(<esm.sh url>)` unreachable to the minifier because it is a runtime string. Bundling would have changed what the network does, not just what the bytes say.

**`.vercelignore` GOVERNS THE UPLOAD, NOT JUST WHAT IS SERVED — and this nearly shipped a dead deploy.** The build script was written into `tools/`, which `.vercelignore` excludes. A build script inside an excluded directory does not exist when the build runs, so `npm run build` fails and the deploy dies. The gitignore-style negation that re-includes one file from an excluded directory (`tools/*` then `!tools/build.js`) does work, but it makes the deploy hinge on a subtlety nobody would think to check. It lives at the repo root instead and unlinks itself at the end, so it is uploaded, runs, and is not served — obviously correct beats cleverly correct.

**esbuild has no HTML loader**, so the 16 markup comments needed a hand-written pass. A bare `<!--[\s\S]*?-->` would also eat anything comment-shaped inside a `<script>` or `<style>` body, including the JSON-LD block, so those are masked out and restored around the strip. Asserted afterwards: zero surviving markers, zero comments, and the JSON-LD still parses.

**`legalComments: 'none'` explicitly.** esbuild's default preserves `/*! */` banners and floats them to the end of the file. Nothing here is licence text, and one surviving banner would be precisely the artefact the request was about.

**Verified by running the suite against the minified output, not by reading it.** `src/` and `public/` were clean in git, so the build ran against the real tree and `git checkout --` restored it exactly: 674/674 pass on minified code, the wager fix survives as `M({...n,wagered:s})`, and the guard was confirmed to refuse a plain `npm run build`. Minification hazards were grepped for first — no `Function.prototype.name`, `toString()`, `eval` or `new Function` anywhere in `src/`.

**What this does NOT buy, said out loud.** Minified code is not obfuscated and the game's logic is still fully readable to anyone who wants it; the deck, the scorer and the EV solver are all still there under short variable names. This is a presentation fix. Nothing about the security posture changed, and nothing should be built on the assumption that the client is now opaque — §11z's server-side re-derivation remains the only thing that matters, and §11am is what makes it true.

---

## 12. Implementation Status

Built so far (`src/core`, `src/state`, `src/ui`, `src/story`, `tools/dev-server.js`): deck/RNG, hand evaluator (with wild-Joker substitution, per-card combo attribution §3h/§3n, a 12-category hand-rank table — the standard 10 plus Three Straight/Four Straight, promoted from extra bonuses to real categories and slotted in by actual rarity, §3w — that's odds-proportional and rank-scaled within each category, reaching into the tens of millions for a Royal Flush, §3s/§3u), EV solver (with an `excludedIndices` option for Locked Card, §4a), full scoring with odds-calibrated, attribution-aware rarity across 5 tiers up to Diamond (flavor + suit synergy + an unconditional Card Value bonus + skill bonuses + the 16-and-growing extra-bonus registry §3f + odds-calibrated card rarity, sharing Bronze/Silver/Gold/Diamond with Joker, that multiplies the whole score only when genuinely part of the winning combo, with a second stacking tier for multiple unused rare cards and a flat +25%-per-extra-rare multiplier kicker §3h/§3m/§3n/§3o/§3p/§3r + a half-credit Discarded Rarity Bonus for rare cards you chose to let go §3r + Pity Points §3i + 9 Daily Modifiers across three passes that multiply (not add to) the total when active, §4a/§4c/§4d/§4e — including Second Look and Double or Nothing, genuinely new game-loop mechanics (a two-round discard/draw, and a 2-card peek into an all-or-nothing gamble) rather than reuses of existing hooks), Luck/Skill/Risk meters (§3g, reworked in §3aa so Skill measures the discard decision and Luck the deck, independently — they used to be two views of the same lucky draw), a 12-archetype personality classifier (§7a, with two fixed Statistician over-triggering bugs — a 0%-Decision-Rating edge case, then a threshold that only required a mediocre discard, not the mathematically optimal one), an 18-achievement catalog with cumulative stats tracking (§7b), one-play-per-day persistence, a playable board with an animated face-down-to-face-up initial deal (§3k) immune to redeal-mid-animation races (§3v), a biggest-number-at-top score reveal (§3j) rendered as RNGDLE-style badge cards with descriptions, highlighted card-proof strips (§3q), and sign-aware +/− formatting for a modifier that can wipe the total (§4e), collapsing to the top badge + a faded half-badge with a Show More/Less toggle once the reveal finishes (§3r), with slower/dramatic flips for rare-card reveals wherever they occur (§3h), a daily-rotating "Caption Builder" — one flowing sentence built from 6 grammatically-roled slots (§6c/§6d) — with a share block, and Daily Modifiers (§4a/§4b/§4c/§4d/§4e) shown via a banner under the day label, with a real "no repeat within 9 days" rotation guarantee and an admin preview picker, and a rarity-roll luck slider (§3o) whose 500× ceiling now actually reaches every tier instead of collapsing to guaranteed bronze. Also built: a site-wide header with a help modal and, once a Supabase project is connected (§11a — schema/client/auth/friends code all built, real end-to-end use pending the owner's own project setup), real magic-link accounts and a friends system. 636 passing unit tests (`npm test`) — the localStorage-backed state modules (`persistence.js`, `stats.js`) are verified via browser smoke tests instead, since plain Node has no `localStorage` global.

Not yet built: the remaining 12 modifiers from §4's full roster (Lowball/Reverse Rankings deliberately deferred — they invert the hand hierarchy other systems depend on), Daily Seed Themes (and the theme→story vocabulary hookup that depends on them), confetti/rare-hand celebration, the Supabase *leaderboard* + Skill Rating specifically (accounts/friends themselves are built, §11a — a leaderboard is a different feature on top of the same backend), XP/levels/streaks/Collection Book UI (§7's remaining bullets).

**Dev-only test mode** (`?test` query param, see `src/state/test-mode.js`) lets a hand be dealt outside the daily seed for local testing — `?test=<seed>` for a reproducible hand, `?test` alone for a new random one each load. It never reads or writes the real daily-result storage, and the page shows an unmissable striped banner whenever it's active, so it can't be mistaken for or leak into a real run.

**Rarity Preview Admin panel** ✅ built (owner request — "a cheater admin mode... maybe a luck slider or something so i can see what the joker and different rarities look like", later extended per "i want the admin page to be more advanced so i can add a certain card/rarity in each of the slots", §3r). Test-mode-only, rendered above the striped banner (`renderAdminPanel()` in `board.js`). Three tools, all trigger a full redeal through the real `startHand()`/`dealInitialHand()` path — same flip/glow/hold animation a genuine reveal gets, not a static mockup:
- **🍀 Luck slider** (1×–500×, bumped up from an initial 60× ceiling per owner follow-up: "i would like for the rarity slider to go higher") scales the COMBINED chance of landing any rarity (`dealHand(seed, count, { luckMultiplier })` in `deck.js` — optional param, defaults to 1/no-op so real daily deals are provably unaffected), preserving each tier's relative share of it — see the bug fix below for why. A live label shows the resulting ~chance-per-card. Paired with a "Redeal with this luck" button rather than redealing on every drag tick, so the animation doesn't replay mid-drag.

  **Bug found and fixed (owner report): "i set the luck slider to 500x and i only get bronze cards, rarely silver gold or diamond."** The original implementation shrank the RAW roll (`roll / luckMultiplier`) before the same cumulative-range lookup real daily deals use — since `RARITIES`' tiers are checked bronze-first and bronze's own cumulative boundary (0.07) is far bigger than any shrunk roll once `luckMultiplier` exceeds ~14× (`1 / 0.07`), the entire shrunk range collapsed to fit inside bronze's slice alone, making bronze the *only* reachable outcome — silver/gold/joker/diamond became mathematically unreachable, and this got *worse*, not better, as luck went up (the opposite of what a luck slider should do). Fixed by rewriting `rarityForRoll(roll, luckMultiplier)` to scale the COMBINED chance of landing any rarity instead (`min(TOTAL_SPECIAL_CHANCE × luckMultiplier, 1)`), then split that total among tiers proportional to their real relative shares (70:30:10:2:1) — the same proportional-renormalization `jokerTierForRoll()` already used for a Joker's own sub-tier. At 500× this correctly saturates to a guaranteed rarity every card (matching the admin panel's own "~100% chance per card" hint text, which had been telling the truth about intent the whole time even though the underlying roll never delivered on it), split roughly 62%/26%/9%/1.8%/0.9% across bronze/silver/gold/joker/diamond. Verified live: 20 redeals at 500× turned up bronze, silver, gold, *and* diamond (joker missed in that particular sample, consistent with its ~1.8% share — a larger sample in the new unit test coverage confirms it's reachable too). 296/296 tests passing (6 new: `rarityForRoll`'s scaling behavior directly, plus an integration-level `dealHand` sample confirming every tier is reachable at high luck).
- **Force buttons** — one per tier (Bronze/Silver/Gold/Diamond) plus one per Joker flavor (also Bronze/Silver/Gold/Diamond, §3h/§3p) — redeal and guarantee that exact rarity (and, for Joker, exact flavor) on one random card slot, independent of the luck slider — the deterministic "show me a Diamond Joker right now" path, for when you don't want to wait on odds at all.
- **🎛️ Custom Hand Builder** (§3r) — 5 independent slot rows, each with its own rank/suit/rarity (and, for Joker, flavor) selects, plus a "Deal This Hand" button that deals exactly that 5-card hand. `buildCustomHand()` sources the draw pile from the rest of the standard deck, shuffled and rarity-rolled with a fresh seed, so post-discard replacements still show real rarities; duplicate rank+suit picks across slots are rejected before dealing.

None of the three touch `dailySeed`/`hashSeed` or persistence — every redeal draws a brand-new random seed (`freshSeed()`), and the day label switches to "admin redeal"/"custom hand builder" wording so it's never mistaken for the reproducible `?test=<seed>` hand the page loaded with.

56. **Profile page, stats, XP and levels — pass 1 of 2** (§11d), owner: "make a full profile page, once you click on the username top right it takes you to your profile with a whole bunch of stats, your 5 recent hands (with a load more to show more) your highest hand, total points, and this is where you can sign out or delete account at the bottom. i also want to impliment unlockable badges you can put next to your name, achivements, unlockable titles... unlockable name paints... i also want a full xp and level system." That's roughly five subsystems, so three things were confirmed before building rather than assumed:
    - **Split into two passes** (owner chose this over one big drop): pass 1 is the page, stats, recent hands, XP/levels, account-backed achievements, and sign out / delete account; pass 2 is the cosmetics layer. Reason for that order specifically — a cosmetic's UNLOCK RULE has to reference something that already exists, so building levels and achievements first means pass 2 hangs off a finished foundation instead of being reworked when the XP design lands.
    - **XP gets its own scale** rather than being derived from lifetime points. Flagged the concrete reason up front: odds-proportional scoring (§3s) makes a Royal Flush worth 54,800,000 against a Pair's 200, so a points-driven curve would let one lucky hand jump a player hundreds of levels. Owner agreed.
    - **Cosmetics split by source**: levels will unlock name paints and titles (predictable progression), achievements will unlock badges (each one proof of a specific thing done) — so neither system is redundant. Recorded here as the contract pass 2 builds against.

    The rest of the design detail is in §11d. Two things worth pulling out as decisions rather than implementation: everything is **derived** from `daily_plays` rather than accumulated into a new table (correct on every device, self-healing, one source of truth — see §11d), and **delete account had to be a `security definer` Postgres function** because the browser's anon key deliberately cannot touch `auth.users`; deleting only the app's own tables client-side would have left a stranded auth user able to sign in to a half-deleted account.

    One investigation worth recording because it looked like a bug and wasn't: the rendered profile screenshot showed a blank gap where one history row should be. Rather than assume it was a screenshot artifact, dumped every row's measured height, date, label and card count — all 12 rows were present, uniform at 56px, with 5 cards each. The gap was a Playwright stitching seam on a 2,516px-tall element capture, not a rendering fault.

    Also renumbered: the new code initially cited "DESIGN.md §12" for the profile, which collided with the existing §12 Implementation Status section. Corrected to §11d across all 7 files that referenced it, keeping it in the accounts family alongside §11a/§11c.

57. **Cosmetics: badges, titles and name paints — pass 2 of 2** (§11e). Built to the contract set in #56, so no new scope questions were needed. Decisions worth recording beyond the implementation:
    - **Badges are derived from the achievement registry** rather than authored as a parallel list, because a parallel list is exactly the kind of thing that drifts silently when an achievement is renamed. Same reasoning as §11d deriving stats from `daily_plays` instead of a second table.
    - **Title placement was mine to choose** (owner: "whichever looks best") — below the name, because titles are phrases long enough to push a name around and wrap badly in a narrow friends row. The site header is the deliberate exception and gets a compact badge-plus-name variant; it is chrome, not a nameplate.
    - **Unlock enforcement is client-side, id validity is server-side**, and §11e spells out why that line sits where it does: shape has to be enforced in the database because these strings render in other users' browsers, but replicating XP and achievement derivation in SQL to enforce *earning* would be a large hand-synced duplication for a stake that is purely cosmetic and cannot touch anyone else's data.
    - **A real bug surfaced only because the header and the profile preview share a screen**: the header caches its profile row, so equipping a cosmetic updated the preview but not the header. Fixed with a window event rather than coupling the two modules.
    - Worth noting for future sessions: one dense mock in the browser test had a brace error that made the fake Supabase module fail to parse, which `getSession()`'s catch turned into a silent "logged out" profile page rather than a visible error. Rewriting the mock's chained builders in multi-line form fixed it. When a mocked-backend test mysteriously renders the signed-out state, suspect the mock's own syntax first.

58. **Admin page — pass 1 of 2** (§11f), owner: "i want an admin page just for one account 'car' which is me to where only i can access it, i want to be able to control everything from it... reset the day, add and remove things to peoples profiles... change the daily modifier and see what day is next... edit the word bank of the poems... search for any profile and edit them while also having a total profile count and active players if that is possible."
    - **Led with the security reality rather than building what was asked literally.** "Only I can access it" is not achievable in browser JavaScript on a static site — a username check there is bypassable by anyone who opens devtools. Said so up front, then built the real boundary in Postgres: `security definer` functions that re-check `is_admin()` server-side, with the client check demoted to deciding what to render. Proved it by patching the client gate away and confirming every action still failed.
    - **Found and closed a privilege-escalation hole created by the feature itself.** `profiles`' UPDATE policy was row-level with no column restriction, so introducing an `is_admin` column would have let any user self-promote. This is the *second* occurrence of this exact pattern (after `daily_plays.seed`, §54), which is why §11f now states it as a standing rule for the project rather than a one-off fix.
    - **Deliberately declined to fake the parts that aren't real yet.** The daily modifier and poem word bank have no server-side state — they're pure client-side functions. Rather than ship a modifier picker that silently did nothing, the schedule is read-only and says why, and the config work is scoped as pass 2. Same reasoning applies to "add customizations like new badges and paints": *granting* existing cosmetics works now; *authoring new ones* needs the same config table.
    - **Owner chose:** foundation-plus-players first; "active" = completed run in the last 7 days (with 24h/30d shown for trend, since real login times aren't reachable from the browser); and admin identity as a SQL-set flag rather than a hardcoded username, so it never enters the public bundle and survives a rename.
    - **A grant would have been inert without a second fix**: the player's own cosmetic picker derived unlocks from level and achievements only, so an admin-granted item appeared locked to its recipient. Caught in verification, fixed with one shared context feeding both the picker and the pre-save re-check.

59. **Server-side game config — admin pass 2 of 2** (§11g). Completes what §11f deliberately left read-only. The decisions worth recording:
    - **core/ was kept pure.** The tempting shortcut was to have `modifiers.js` and `generator.js` read config directly. Instead config is threaded in as optional trailing arguments, which is why the remote-dependent behaviour could still get 36 unit tests and why every existing call site kept working unchanged.
    - **`game_config` has no write policy at all**, only a SELECT one. Combined with `security definer` setter functions, that leaves exactly one authorized write path instead of two (a policy AND a function) that could drift apart.
    - **Validation runs on read, not just on write.** The admin page refusing bad input isn't sufficient protection when a row could predate a shape change or be hand-edited — and this table is read by every client at boot, so a single bad row would otherwise be a global outage. Reading falls back to built-in defaults, and partial validity is preserved rather than discarding a whole map for one bad entry.
    - **Word bank overrides are partial on purpose**, so untouched slots keep receiving future built-in content rather than silently freezing at whatever shipped the day the admin first saved.
    - **Custom paints are hex-only as a security decision.** They become inline styles in other players' browsers, so the strict-hex validator is the CSS-injection guard, and it is tested with real attack strings. Gradients/animation would need authored CSS, so they stay code-only — a limitation stated in the UI rather than worked around.
    - **A performance regression I introduced, then measured and fixed.** Gating the Draw button on the config fetch (to guarantee overrides applied before dealing) delayed it from ~117ms to ~960ms. The requirement was only "before cards are dealt", not "before the button appears", so the await moved to the click handler — instant button, guarantee preserved, both re-measured.

60. **Cosmetic availability modes** (§11h), owner bug report: "i just added an owner title and now everyone has it." A genuine design flaw in what I shipped in §11g, not a misuse — custom titles and paints could only be level-gated, and since every player is level 1 or higher, a level-1 title was a title for everybody. The fix adds `everyone` and `grant` (locked/hidden) alongside `level`.
    - **The non-obvious half was making re-locking actually work.** Adding the mode is easy; the flaw only truly closes if flipping a cosmetic to grant-only REMOVES it from players who already equipped it while it was open to all. `resolveEquipped()` now drops a grant-mode cosmetic unless the profile row carries the grant.
    - **Scoped honestly:** that check works for `grant` mode because ownership is exactly `admin_unlocks`, which is on the profile row and therefore available anywhere a nameplate renders — including a friend's row with no play history. Level- and achievement-gated ownership can't be judged there, so `ownsGrantOnly()` deliberately only answers the question it can answer, and the limit is documented rather than papered over.
    - **Defaults preserve existing behaviour** (badges `grant`, titles/paints `level`), so nothing already authored silently changes meaning; and a custom badge's `level` collapses to `grant` rather than creating an unreachable cosmetic.
    - Verified by reproducing the reported bug in a browser first — author "Owner" at level 1, confirm any player can equip it — then confirming the fix removes it from both the picker and the already-equipped nameplate.
    - Incidental cleanup the work exposed: the custom-cosmetic Remove button shared the `.admin-select-btn` class ("select a player"), and a test duly clicked the wrong one. Renamed to `.admin-remove-btn`.

61. **Owner punch-list, part 1 of 2** (§11i). Seven items were pasted at once; three needed decisions and were asked about rather than guessed:
    - **"different rarities for higher scores" was genuinely ambiguous** — a grade for the whole run, new card tiers above Diamond, or existing rare cards scoring more. Asked; the answer was a run grade. Building the wrong one of those three would have been a lot of wasted work.
    - **Leaderboards and clickable friend profiles turned out to need the same thing** (permission to read other players' scores), so they were surfaced as one linked decision instead of two, and deferred to a single migration pass (§11j). Owner chose: daily + weekly + all-time top scores plus all-time career points, with a friends-only toggle, and completed runs readable by anyone.
    - **Two of the seven were bugs I had introduced**, and both are worth remembering as patterns. `createProfile` assumed one meaning for a Postgres error code that has two (unique constraint on `id` AND on `username` both raise 23505) — the fix asks the database what is true rather than parsing error text. And the header painted a logged-OUT state before it knew, because its first synchronous render predates the async session answer; the general lesson is that "render immediately so nothing is blank" needs a third state for *unknown*, not a default to one of the two known ones.
    - **An existing test earned its keep**: it caught that moving the config await into the Draw click left the button with no immediate feedback. Fixed, and the now-outdated assertion was rewritten rather than deleted.

62. **Score grade ladder renamed, with grade rewards** (§11k), owner: the nine names, "also make badges titles and achievements for earning these". Points worth keeping:
    - **All three reward systems are generated from the ladder**, not authored alongside it. Nine grades produced nine achievements, nine badges (free, since badges already derive from achievements) and nine titles with no parallel lists to keep in sync — the same discipline as §11e.
    - **The floor grade needed different logic from the rest.** Every other tier is "reach it or better" so a good run cascades; "Busted or better" would be every run ever, so it is exact-match — a wooden spoon.
    - **"???" required a deliberate reachability decision.** Set at 4× a Royal Flush so it needs the best hand AND a multiplier, rather than being a second name for Impossible. Tested from both directions: unreachable on a bare Royal, reachable with a real modifier or rare card.
    - **Titles gained a second unlock source.** Grade titles unlock from an achievement, not a level, because a level threshold cannot express "you scored like a Whale". Built-in titles now support an optional `achievementId`.
    - **A duplicate label ("High Roller" as both a level-8 title and a grade) was caught and resolved** by relabelling the older one to Big Spender, keeping its id so nobody loses it — plus a new test forbidding duplicate title labels.
    - Two browser assertions failed on my own wrong assumptions rather than on the code (CSS `text-transform` changing `innerText` casing, and a 410-XP run being level 2 not level 1). Both are recurring traps in this project's tests and are now noted here.

63. **Leaderboards and viewable profiles** (§11j) — the last two punch-list items, deliberately built as one pass.
    - **They were surfaced as a single linked decision** rather than two separate features, because both were blocked on the same thing: `daily_plays` was own-rows-only. Asking "how open should scores be?" once answered both, and the owner's choice (completed runs readable by anyone) is what makes a global board possible at all.
    - **The read policy is narrow on purpose.** `result is not null` isn't tidiness — an unfinished row holds the seed for a hand not yet locked in, so a blanket `using (true)` would have let anyone preview another player's day. Worth remembering that this table has two very different kinds of row in it.
    - **Three boards, one function.** Today / This Week / All-Time differ only by a date cutoff, so they share one SQL function; only career points, which aggregates, needed its own.
    - **Chose `security invoker` over `security definer`** for the board functions, unlike every admin function in the project. The distinction is worth recording: admin functions elevate because they must do things the caller cannot, whereas these read exactly what the caller already may — so not elevating keeps them automatically correct if the policy changes.
    - **Friends-only filtering is client-side**, because friendship rows are already restricted to their two participants and the alternative was duplicating a join into every board function.
    - **Viewing another player reuses the same profile page** with an edit flag, rather than a second read-only page that could drift. Three edge cases needed explicit handling: your own username via URL (must stay editable), an unknown username, and signed-out visitors.
    - **A layout regression I introduced was caught by three unrelated suites' 375px checks** — the new header button overflowed mobile. Those overflow assertions have now earned their place twice.
64. **Game day moves to 7pm New York, plus three presentation items** (§11l), owner: "add a timer to the next draw reset, it should happen at 7pm est".
    - **"EST" was taken as intent, not as a literal offset.** Literal EST is a fixed −05:00, which would drift to 8pm local in summer. Asked; the owner confirmed 7pm *New York* year-round. Worth remembering that timezone abbreviations in a request usually name a place, not an offset.
    - **The reset lives in one pure module and nothing computes "today" on its own any more.** Seven files were open-coding `toISOString().slice(0,10)`. Once the boundary stops being midnight UTC, a single straggler means two parts of the app disagree about which puzzle is live — so consolidating was the prerequisite, not a tidy-up.
    - **Skipping the data migration was verified, not assumed.** In winter 19:00 EST *is* midnight UTC, so the new labelling is byte-identical to the old for existing rows; only the 23:00–00:00 UTC hour in summer shifts. There is a test whose whole job is to hold that property, because it is the justification for leaving stored `play_date` values untouched.
    - **The DB got the same rule.** A SQL `game_today()` mirrors the client, used by the admin functions and the board windows — otherwise "today" would differ between client and server for five hours a day.
    - **The countdown shows seconds because it ticks every second.** A once-a-minute value updated 60 times a minute isn't a timer, it's a static label with wasted writes. At zero it reloads rather than hot-swapping the day, since seed/modifier/caption state is all derived at load.
    - **Extracted the mini-card renderer instead of copying it.** The leaderboard and the profile history must look identical; two copies drift.
    - **An accessibility constraint made the visual design better.** Vivid fills put white text at 2.0–3.0:1, and the 0.6rem compact badge needs 4.5:1, not 3:1. Splitting each tier into a darkened contrast-safe *fill* plus an undarkened *glow* for the rim gave a badge that pops more than the bright flat fill would have. The darkening was computed by binary search per stop rather than eyeballed.
    - **A screenshot lied again — third time.** Badges looked washed out because they were captured mid-fade-in (`line-in`, opacity 0→1, fill-mode `both`), not because the CSS was wrong. Measure the DOM; wait for animations before capturing.
    - **A patch script without a match assertion silently did nothing**, leaving `profile.js` using `miniCardHtml` with no import. Every scripted edit needs to assert it matched — the sibling patches in the same script did, and landed.
65. **A granted cosmetic that rendered as nothing** (§11m), owner: "i added a title as a locked/hidden and i can select it to equip but it doesnt show on my name" — plus three top-bar changes.
    - **The bug was in the callers, not the cosmetic system.** `resolveEquipped` needs two things to render a grant-only custom cosmetic: the registry it lives in, and proof the player was granted it. `profile.js` rebuilt a nameplate row without `admin_unlocks`; `header.js` passed no `custom` at all. Either one alone is enough to make the cosmetic silently disappear.
    - **The dangerous property is the silence.** The check is correct and protects a real thing (re-locking an over-permissive cosmetic must take it back). What made it a bug magnet is that its input is easy to omit and omitting it fails quietly toward "you don't own this". Same shape as the login flash: an unknown value treated as a known one.
    - **Fixed by removing the opportunity**, not by patching four call sites: one `NAMEPLATE_COLUMNS` constant defines what a row needs to draw a name, and every select uses it. Tests pin the trap in both directions because the failure looks like a broken cosmetic rather than a broken fetch.
    - **Confirmed with the owner that titles stay off the top bar.** They are phrases, the bar is one line; they render everywhere else. Asked rather than assumed, since "doesn't show on my name" could have meant either surface.
    - **Rename shares the signup rule** rather than defining a second one, and records why 23505 is unambiguous on UPDATE (the id isn't changing) where it was ambiguous on INSERT.
    - **A latent bug surfaced on the way:** the header replaced its cached profile with the event payload, but callers announce partial rows. Merging, not assigning.
    - **All caps in CSS, and it did not work at first.** `text-transform` is inherited, so setting it on the bar looked sufficient — but the UA stylesheet declares `text-transform: none` on form controls, and an explicit declaration beats an inherited one whatever its origin, so every button ignored it. Cousin of the `[hidden]` trap: a UA default outranking a rule that looks like it should win. Worth checking computed style on the actual element, not the ancestor you set the property on.
    - **Uppercasing is presentation only** — the stored username keeps its casing, so it still renders as typed on the profile page and the boards, and screen readers get the real text.
66. **Uppercase usernames, reveal order, leaderboard polish, animated paints, header centring, profile hero** (§11n).
    - **Uppercasing NAMES is a data change, not the CSS change §11m made.** Migration 009 refuses rather than guesses: `username` is unique case-sensitively, so 'Card' and 'card' can coexist today and would collide. It raises with the offending groups and changes nothing — renaming someone's account unasked is worse than stopping.
    - **The lookups were the easy half to forget.** Stored 'CAR' is not found by `.eq('username','car')`, so a previously-shared `?profile=car` link and a lower-case friend request would both silently fail. One `normalizeUsername` sits on every write *and* every exact-match read.
    - **`toUpperCase()`, deliberately not `toLocaleUpperCase()`** — the locale-aware one maps Turkish 'i' to 'İ', outside the schema's ASCII pattern, so "iris" would have been rejected by the CHECK constraint. Pinned by a test.
    - **The reveal bug was two defects.** The discard path used `Promise.all` + stagger while the deal path used a serialized loop, so rarity-dependent durations decided the landing order; and the rarity-paced duration was applied to the turn-to-*back*, putting the drama on the card being discarded. The owner's description named the second one precisely.
    - **The header looked off *because* of the uppercasing.** Uppercase has no descenders, so line-box centring leaves the descender gap below the glyphs. Fixed by centring on the ink. Measured with canvas `TextMetrics`, because a `Range` rect includes the line box and the trailing letter-space and can see neither problem.
    - **For aligned columns, the FLEXIBLE track must be the last one.** Putting `1fr` on the name let a 900,000,000 score widen the score column, steal from the name, and shift that row's hand 19px. Fixed tracks left of the hand, flex on the right.
    - **A shared `.grade-pill` so a rarity's colours exist once.** And when the grade pill was removed from the board, its *name* moved to `title`/`aria-label` — colour is not something a screen reader can convey.
    - **Only top tiers animate their highlight.** 25 shimmering rows signal nothing. Reduced motion slows animated cosmetics to 24s rather than killing them, because motion is the point of the tier.
    - **A generic element selector reached a component.** `header { text-align: center }`, written for the board heading, was centring `.profile-header` — that is what "out a little" was.
    - **`[hidden]` trap, SIXTH time**, and the worst instance yet: an explicit `display` on header buttons un-hid the Admin button for everyone. Caught only because the admin suite asserts a non-admin never sees the link. New rule of thumb: never set `display` on a selector in this codebase without asking what toggles `hidden` on it.
    - **A test sweep that counts passes hides crashes.** Two suites reported "0 fail" while dying part-way through; a crashed suite has no failures. The sweep now checks exit codes and greps for `TimeoutError`, and immediately caught a second crashing suite I had already accepted as green.
67. **Hand names on the boards, two-phase discard, badge metrics, and the mobile login fix** (§11o).
    - **The hand's name is derived from the stored cards, never stored.** Same rule as the career stats: the cards are the record, and a second copy of what they already say can only drift. Proven genuinely evaluated by a row whose label ("Four of a Kind") only exists once the wild is resolved.
    - **…and guarded, because `evaluateHand` throws.** `final_hand` is whatever the database holds; a malformed historical row must lose its own label, not blank the whole board.
    - **"Turn them all over, then reveal" is a different shape from §11n's fix, not a bigger dose of it.** §11n stopped rare cards overtaking common ones *within* one interleaved cascade; this separates the turn-over into its own completed phase, so the discard reads as one beat instead of three.
    - **The turn-over pass is parallel and the reveal pass is serial, on purpose.** Every turn-to-back runs at the same duration, and with identical durations start order *is* land order — so a staggered cascade is still exactly left-to-right. The reveal cannot do that; its durations vary by rarity, which was §11n's original bug.
    - **Compose the animation, don't copy it.** `flipCardToBack` is the first half of `flipReplaceCard`, which already skipped its turn-to-back for an element that was face-down — so the two still add up to the identical motion instead of becoming two drifting copies.
    - **"Align badge emojis" was not a horizontal problem.** Advance widths were already identical and names already started at one x. The defect was the emoji's ink being ~55% taller than the caps and hanging 3px *below the baseline* — measured, not eyeballed. Uppercase names expose it, exactly as in §11n's header bug.
    - **A `transform` moves ink without moving layout**, which is what makes the lift safe — but it applies only because `.nameplate-line` is `inline-flex` and blockifies its children. Transforms are ignored on true inline boxes.
    - **A 1Hz label should not re-parse its own markup.** The countdown's time element is built once and only its text changes; rebuilding it each tick would recreate the same nodes 3,600 times an hour.
    - **Check the SDK's actual defaults before diagnosing its behaviour.** Probing the real esm.sh build gave `flowType: "implicit"`, which rules out PKCE and therefore rules out "a link opened in another browser cannot work" — narrowing the possible causes to one. I had reasoned from memory first.
    - **"Login doesn't save" is the one auth bug no link can fix**, and it is narrower than it sounds: only mail apps with an *isolated* in-app webview (Gmail on iOS, Outlook mobile). iOS Mail hands off to the default browser and Android Gmail uses Custom Tabs, which share Chrome's storage. No site can write into another browser's storage; that is a hard boundary, not a missing feature.
    - **The 6-digit code was the wrong fix, rejected on product grounds.** It answered a case that may not be the one biting, and made the common path ambiguous by putting a button and a code in the same email. Kept as an uncalled function with the reasoning attached rather than deleted, because it is still the only answer to the webview case.
    - **`{{ .ConfirmationURL }}` is a GET endpoint that redeems on request** — so every scanner, filter and prefetcher that follows links burns the sign-in before the player taps it. `{{ .TokenHash }}` on our own domain is inert to them, because redeeming it needs JavaScript. This is the more common failure *and* the fixable one.
    - **A top-level `await` is only safe when it is gated and cannot throw.** The callback awaits a CDN-backed module — §11a's brick — so it runs only when `token_hash` is present and swallows every error. A test with esm.sh aborted proves the game still loads.
    - **Go templates render HTML comments too.** Naming `.ConfirmationURL` in the template's own explanatory comment would have put a live single-use sign-in URL into every email's source, where a URL-extracting scanner could consume it — recreating the exact bug the file exists to prevent.
    - **The likeliest cause is config nobody can assert from code.** `emailRedirectTo` is ignored unless the origin is in the Redirect URLs allow-list, and Supabase then falls back to Site URL silently — a leftover `localhost` Site URL breaks phones while laptops work. Written into `supabase/email-templates/` next to the markup, since a dashboard is not version control.
    - **A harness race that looked exactly like a product bug.** The reveal sampler polled at 20ms while `waitForFunction` polled on rAF, so the last card's reveal could go unobserved and the ordering assertion failed for purely test-side reasons. Check the measurement before believing the measurement.
68. **Friend discovery, duplicate names, and the paint seam** (§11p).
    - **"Multiple of the same name" was two unrelated bugs.** The leaderboard ranked PLAYS rather than players, so a good week filled several rows with one person; and `friendships`' unique constraint is DIRECTIONAL, so A→B and B→A could both exist and render one friendship as two people. Worth checking whether a vague bug report has more than one cause before declaring it fixed.
    - **A `limit` must sit outside a de-duplicating subquery.** Inside, it takes the top N rows and *then* collapses them, quietly returning fewer than N players.
    - **Uniqueness over an unordered pair needs an expression index** — `unique (least(a,b), greatest(a,b))`. A column-pair constraint only ever constrains the direction it is written in.
    - **Fix the data AND the render.** Migration 010 removes the duplicate rows, but `getFriends` dedupes anyway: a browser can be talking to a database that has not run the migration, and the list should not depend on a constraint holding to be correct.
    - **Search-then-click removes the state that caused the bug.** Offering "Add" to somebody who has already asked you is exactly how the reciprocal duplicate got made; showing "Accept" instead makes it unreachable from the UI.
    - **Prefix, not substring, and `like` not `ilike`.** Only a prefix can use a b-tree index, and since names are stored uppercase and the query is normalized, the case-sensitive operator is both correct and the indexable one.
    - **Debounce plus a sequence number.** Debouncing alone still lets a slow early response overwrite a later, more specific one.
    - **The paint seam was arithmetic, not taste.** Percentage `background-position` resolves against (container − image), so travel per 100% is (s−1) box widths while a tile is s — seamless needs `sweep = 100% × s/(s−1)`. Standardising on `s = 2` makes the two coupled numbers both 200% and hard to get wrong.
    - **A comment can assert a property the code does not have.** The stylesheet claimed the loop was seamless because the gradient's first and last stops matched — a real requirement, but a different one. Matching stops hide the join in SPACE; only the tile arithmetic hides it in TIME.
    - **A fake that fails to PARSE is silent.** A bad regex in a test's fake Supabase client made the dynamic import reject, which `supabase-client.js` swallows by design — so every page rendered logged-out with no error at all, and three unrelated sections "broke". `node --check` on the extracted template found it in seconds; guessing did not.
    - **Playwright truncates the locator in a timeout log.** I spent several minutes fixing the click I had just changed when the failure was an earlier one.
69. **The pinned modifier that un-pinned itself** (§11q).
    - **"It resets when a version is pushed" was not about deploys at all.** The override lives in `game_config`; a push just prompts the reload where the reset becomes visible. Correlation the reporter offers is a clue, not a diagnosis.
    - **The game day and the UTC date agree in winter and disagree for one hour a day in summer.** That is the worst possible failure shape — it works for months, then misbehaves in a narrow window nobody is watching, and only during EDT.
    - **§11l claimed this cleanup was complete and it was not.** Two of the nine `toISOString().slice(0,10)` sites survived. A note saying "all now call `gameDayFor`" is only true until the next one is written; a grep is worth more than the claim.
    - **Adding 86,400,000ms is not "the next day".** Across a DST change it shifts the New York wall clock by an hour and can cross the 19:00 boundary, duplicating or skipping a day. Step the LABEL, which has no timezone.
    - **Midnight UTC is the wrong instant to represent a game day** — in summer it is 20:00 the previous evening, already past the reset. Noon UTC round-trips correctly every day of the year.
    - **Revert the fix and watch the test fail.** A regression test that has never been seen red is an assumption, not a test. This one caught 1 unit and 2 browser assertions.
    - **A stale comment is a live hazard.** `dailySeed`'s claim that the other daily rotations "derive their seed the same way" stopped being true in §11l, and is a plausible route by which the UTC convention got copied into the override lookup.
70. **Four scoring modifiers, a leaderboard modifier, one removal, and friends-panel names** (§4f, §11r).
    - **Held Card is Locked Card inverted, and the visual language has to say so.** Same "mark a slot" shape, opposite meaning — one forbids discarding, the other bribes you not to. Opposite corner, solid ring instead of dashed, and deliberately no dimming or disabled state.
    - **"Did you keep it?" is not answerable from the final hand.** A replacement occupies the same index, so the scoring callback needed `discardIndices`. Added as an optional context bag rather than more positional arguments, so nothing existing had to change.
    - **One parameterised modifier beats twelve near-identical ones.** Hot Hand names a different category each day from the seeded RNG, the shape Suit Bonus already established — twelve entries would have made hand-type days two-thirds of the roster.
    - **Exclude the categories a bonus cannot pay out on.** High Card scores 0 (multiplying it stays 0) and Royal Flush is too rare to land; either would produce a day indistinguishable from having no modifier.
    - **Exact category match, not "or better".** Flush Frenzy spans every flush because Straight and Royal Flush ARE flushes. Hot Hand has no such family, so "Two Pair day" means Two Pair.
    - **A reverse-ranked board CANNOT be built client-side.** The SQL sends the top N by score DESC; reversing those gives the bottom of the top 25, not the true lowest — the rows you need were never sent. Sorting has to precede the limit, which means it has to happen in SQL.
    - **Containment is what made Lowball shippable.** §12 deferred it because achievements, personality, the decision rating and the EV solver all assume higher is better. Flipping only the board's ORDER touches none of them, and still creates the intended tension against career points.
    - **Clamp a per-day rule to the per-day board.** `fetchLeaderboard` refuses `ascending` on the window boards rather than trusting callers — one day's modifier must not reorder scores from days it has nothing to do with.
    - **An async config read can clobber a synchronous user action.** The server override landed a second after a tester picked a preview and silently replaced it. An explicit action now outranks a fetch already in flight.
    - **A browser suite that is not hermetic will lie to you.** These tests were reaching the real Supabase project, where a pinned modifier overwrote the previewed one — which looked exactly like the preview button being broken.
    - **An unstyled anchor is not neutral.** The friends panel had no rule for its name link, so it inherited the UA's hyperlink blue — which is why only UNPAINTED names looked wrong while painted ones were fine. `color: inherit` is the fix; hardcoding white (as literally requested) would have been invisible on the light theme.
71. **Result panel ending, header spacing, and Share** (§11s).
    - **Two code paths rendering "the same" panel will drift.** The live reveal stacked the hand name above the score; the already-played reload collapsed them onto one line. Now identical markup, with the layout test running against BOTH.
    - **A bare element selector reached a component again.** `header { margin-bottom: 2rem }`, written for the board header, was also padding the sticky site bar — 32px of the 44px gap the owner asked to cut. Same shape as §11n's `header { text-align: center }` hitting `.profile-header`.
    - **Moving a button out of a hidden container makes it clickable too early.** Share used to be inside the story block and therefore unclickable until the fortune rendered; moved out, it was visible seconds before its handler existed. Anything relocated out of a deferred container needs its own reveal.
    - **`[hidden]` trap, SEVENTH time** — `.share-row { display: flex }` needed its `[hidden]` guard. Written at the same moment as the `display`, which is the habit that finally stops this recurring.
    - **Reach the state for real when you can.** The already-played assertions come from actually replaying and reloading, so they would catch a regression in the persistence path too, not just in the markup.
    - **A moving control beats no control, but loses to a well-placed static one.** Sticky met both stated constraints and was still rejected: predictability matters more than reachability once a fixed spot exists that is above the fold anyway. The fix was to question the assumption that Share belonged in the result panel at all.
    - **Geometry assertions break when an element's positioning changes.** "Share is below the come-back line" was true of the layout and false of the on-screen box once sticky; the assertions now check same-row membership and computed `position`, which survive either arrangement.
    - **Moving an element is a chance to notice a duplicate.** The sign-off existed twice on the already-played screen. Relocating Share next to one of them made that obvious and removed the reason for the second.
72. **Header XP bar, mobile hamburger, and the XP flight** (§11t).
