    - **When a documented rationale blocks a request, look for a bounded proxy.** XP excluded points because they span 274,000x. The score GRADE is nine tiers, so it delivers "more points, more XP" while leaving that reasoning intact — and it matches the grade the player was just shown.
    - **"Derived, not accumulated" has a cost, and the cost lands on whoever displays it.** The header cannot read a level; it must fetch history and recompute. So the fetch is async (the bar appears late rather than blocking the name) and cached per page load (the profile page needs the same rows).
    - **Decide what stays OUT of a mobile menu before building it.** The nameplate and XP bar stay visible because the XP animation needs a target and the bar should be glanceable; a pill flying into a closed hamburger communicates nothing.
    - **One set of buttons, restacked — never a second copy.** Duplicating the nav into the dropdown would mean applying every `hidden` rule twice, and getting that wrong is how the Admin button once showed to everyone.
    - **Let the media query own visibility.** The menu reappears on resize with no listener, because only the open/closed state is a class; the breakpoint decides the rest.
    - **A block element's box is not its text.** Positioning the pill against `.score-total`'s right edge put it at the panel border, because the block is full-width and the digits are centred. A `Range` over the contents measures the ink.
    - **A -50% transform means the coordinate you set is the CENTRE.** Half the element's width has to be added to clear what it is meant to sit beside — and the result clamped so it cannot leave the viewport.
    - **Colour carries meaning that is already spoken for.** The pill was amber at first because `--selected` was to hand; that is the card-selection colour. `--accent` green both matches the request and ties the pill to the bar it flies into.
    - **Order the final flourish after everything that changes its number.** Achievements award XP, so the pill fires after that branch or it shows a figure that is already stale.
    - **A test encoding an owner request still needs re-reading, not just re-passing.** "The nameplate is the last child" guarded against the name shifting per-player. A hamburger that is present at every width does not do that — so the assertion was rewritten to the intent rather than deleted or blindly satisfied.
73. **The bar actually filling, level-ups, and a local test account** (§11u).
    - **Re-rendering a container kills every transition inside it.** `renderAuthSlot()` replaced the fill element, so it was born at its final width with nothing to animate from. Mutate the node that is on screen. The value was correct throughout, which is why this survived a passing test suite.
    - **Assert the MECHANISM, not just the endpoint.** "The bar shows the right percentage" passed the entire time it was broken. "The fill node is never replaced" and "it passes through intermediate widths" are what actually catch it.
    - **A level-up must not look like the bar going backwards.** Crossing a level resets progress to near zero, so fill to full and flash FIRST, then start the new level from empty.
    - **`transition: none` then a width then restoring it needs a forced layout between.** Otherwise the browser coalesces the writes and the rewind never happens.
    - **Reduced motion is not "remove all movement".** The flash and pop go; the bar still fills, because a progress bar that teleports has lost the thing it exists to show.
    - **Two independent gates, and a fabricated identity that grants nothing.** The fake account needs `?test` AND a deliberate flag, and holds no Supabase session — so at worst it lies to one browser. A test proves the flag alone does nothing.
    - **Synthesise fixtures from real inputs, not from the answer.** The fake history is built from run RESULTS so `derivePlayerStats` does its real work; a stated total would have tested nothing. A base-only run is worth exactly 100, which makes any target reachable on the nose.
    - **A dev tool has to actually move.** Test mode never persists, so the fake account was frozen — runs now bank their XP into it, or "watch the bar fill" was untestable, which was the entire request.
    - **"Works with no backend" is not the same condition as "works".** Every fake-account test blocked esm.sh, so Supabase's `onAuthStateChange(INITIAL_SESSION, null)` never fired and never overwrote the fake session — the exact bug the owner then hit. A stub that is REACHABLE and reports nobody signed in is the case that mattered.
    - **Two sources of identity will disagree.** `getSession()` returned the fake session while the subscription said null, and the header simply believed whichever spoke last. Anything faking identity has to intercept every channel that reports it, not just the one that is convenient.
    - **`prefers-reduced-motion` means reduce, not delete.** Swapping the XP flight for a fade-in-place removed the very thing the animation exists to say. Keep the journey, drop the bounce, scale and speed. Judged twice now — the bar's fill made the same call.
    - **Test the accessibility branch, not just the default one.** Nothing exercised `reduce`, so a whole class of users got a broken feature that every suite called passing. Playwright takes `reducedMotion` per context; there is no excuse for not covering it.
74. **Support footer** (§11v).
    - **A branded third-party button is a remote image.** Ad blockers stop it by default, so the "support" link would be an empty box for a good share of visitors. A styled text link always renders, and keeps the project's one-external-dependency rule intact.
    - **`target="_blank"` without `rel="noopener"` hands the opened tab a handle back to yours.** Cheap to get right, invisible when wrong.
    - **`margin-top: auto` on the last child of a `100vh` flex column** is what pins a footer to the bottom on short pages without pinning it over content on long ones.
75. **Wild becomes a card property, not a rarity tier** (§3x).
    - **Two ideas sharing one field means neither can vary independently.** Wild occupied the rarity slot, so a card could be wild OR rare, never both. Splitting the axis made "Gold Wild" expressible at all.
    - **Raising a thing's frequency means lowering its per-instance payoff.** Wilds went from ~1 hand in 100 to ~1 in 11, so the multiplier came off and a flat bonus replaced it. Frequency and reward are one decision, not two.
    - **Keep the RNG call count identical when changing what a roll MEANS.** Two calls per card before and after, so every seed still deals the same ranks and suits — the change stayed contained to what those cards rolled.
    - **Stored rows outlive the model that wrote them.** Every saved hand records its wild as `rarity: 'joker'`, and the leaderboard re-evaluates those rows to name the hand. One `isWild()` reading both shapes is what stops history silently rewriting itself.
    - **When a category stops being a category, everything keyed off it needs revisiting** — reveal pacing was a lookup by rarity id, and wild was one of them. It now takes the slower of the two sources.
    - **Writing the test caught the copy lying.** The help text claimed a wild pays nothing, which was true of the design I assumed and false of the one chosen. Documentation is an assertion; test it like one.
    - **A new button with a new attribute needs the listener's selector widened.** "Show Wild" had only `data-force-wild` while the handler bound `[data-force]` — it rendered and did nothing.
76. **Discoverability, progression pacing, and three silent failures** (§11w).
    - **A fixed rate of play makes any super-linear curve a wall.** One run per day is a hard ceiling, so a band growing as `300 × L` doesn't pace progress, it ends it. Capping the band is what keeps levels arriving forever.
    - **Retuning a DERIVED value re-levels everyone on their next load.** That is only safe in one direction. Every new band being ≤ the old one at the same level means nobody can be demoted — and a test pins that against the replaced curve rather than trusting the arithmetic.
    - **Anchor to the constant, never to its current value.** `test-account.js` hardcoded `100` in four places because that was `XP_BASE_PER_RUN` at the time. Raising the base silently desynced the entire fake account. The same rule the score thresholds already follow.
    - **One `catch` for two unrelated failures will misdiagnose both.** "Bad token" and "the SDK never downloaded" arrived at the same handler and were reported as the former, which sent weeks of debugging at the wrong thing. A distinct error type is the fix, and the message it produces is the actual deliverable.
    - **Don't destroy the evidence on a failure you didn't diagnose.** Stripping `token_hash` unconditionally burned a still-valid link whenever the network hiccuped, making every retry — and every fresh email — fail identically.
    - **A dependency range pins nothing, including its DEFAULTS.** `@2` let esm.sh UA-sniff a build target and left `flowType` free to change under us; PKCE would break the mail-app handoff silently. Pin the version, the target, and any default you actually rely on.
    - **Ad blockers match selectors, not text.** `share-btn` as both class and id matched generic cosmetic rules from either direction, and `display: none !important` beats `hidden = false` — invisible, with no error. Rename the selector; the visible label can keep the word.
    - **A null element is not the only blocked-button failure.** A `:remove()` rule deletes the node, so every use needs guarding — one unguarded `shareBtn.hidden = false` aborted the whole already-played render.
    - **"It didn't save" is worth verifying before fixing.** The write, the day key and the override were all correct; the bug was a re-render silently reverting the dropdown and then reporting success. Reading the live row first saved rewriting a working path.
    - **A read that cannot fail will happily report emptiness as success.** `loadGameConfig` never rejects by design — right for the game, dangerous for an editor, where an empty config looks like a lost save and arms the next one to wipe everything. `loaded` is the flag that separates them.
    - **Never cache a failed fetch alongside successful ones.** One blip at boot poisoned the config for the whole session, and the only symptom was the wrong modifier.
    - **A re-render can outrun an animation, and the fallback made it likelier.** Adding a second CDN delayed `loadGameConfig`, whose `.then` re-rendered the auth slot mid-flight and replaced the element the XP bar was transitioning. Deferring the render while an animation owns the DOM is the general fix; the test caught it because it asserts the MECHANISM.
    - **A stub that blocks one origin no longer means "offline."** Adding a fallback CDN silently gave seven "unreachable" suites a live network. When production gains a path, every test that simulates its absence has to gain it too.
    - **Polling loses races that observers don't.** Playwright's `waitForSelector` found the XP pill and cleared the 25ms timer before it ever ticked, so a demonstrably-present element read as absent. `MutationObserver` fires off the DOM change itself — the same lesson already learned for `requestAnimationFrame`.
    - **A crawler sees the pre-JavaScript page.** For a client-rendered app that means `<head>` is the whole story, and the origin-root files (`robots.txt`, `sitemap.xml`) must sit where the static root actually is — not next to the HTML.
77. **Username blocklist** (§11x).
    - **A validation rule the client owns is not a rule.** `profiles` is reachable straight from the browser with a public key, so the word check had to be a trigger. Third time this project has arrived at "the database is the rule, the client is the courtesy" — after the username pattern and the `is_admin` column grant.
    - **Guard every column that can be WRITTEN, not just created.** `username` is user-updatable, so an INSERT-only check would have been defeated by signing up clean and renaming a second later.
    - **World-readable is a property of the table, not of the data you put in it.** `game_config` was the obvious home and would have published the list. A table with RLS on and no policies at all is unreachable over REST for everyone, which is what a private list actually needs.
    - **Normalisation is where the difficulty lives, and it cuts both ways.** Folding leetspeak and padding is what stops `5L_UR`; the same folding is what makes `ASS` match `CLASSIC`. Tiered matching plus an explicit allow list is the price of folding aggressively.
    - **If two rules mean different things, they need different normalisation.** One folder served both tiers and was wrong for each in opposite directions — too aggressive for `exact` (`Card_le_99` became `CARDLE`), and the naive fix would have been too lax for `substring` (`N9I9G9G9E9R` stops matching). "Does this appear in here" and "is the whole thing this" are not the same question.
96. **Comments off the wire, not out of the repo** (§11an).
    - **A literal reading of a request can destroy the thing that makes the project maintainable.** "Remove all the comments" and "stop shipping the comments" look like the same ask and are opposites. The build step answers it; deleting them from source would have thrown away the record that a trigger holds §11z up.
    - **A no-build project has no separation between source and bundle, and that is a publishing decision nobody made.** Serving `src/` directly meant every internal note was a devtools click away. It was never decided, it was inherited from `outputDirectory: "."`.
    - **Probe what is exposed before deciding what to fix.** `.vercelignore` was already hiding DESIGN.md, the migrations and the tests. Assuming otherwise would have produced a much larger and entirely unnecessary change.
    - **`.vercelignore` controls the UPLOAD, not just what is served.** A build script placed in an excluded directory does not exist when the build runs — a dead deploy, not a degraded one. Obviously-correct placement beats a gitignore negation that works but nobody would verify.
    - **A build that rewrites its own checkout must refuse to run on yours.** Ephemeral CI checkout makes in-place minification free and path-change-free; the same script against a working tree is destructive. Guard on the CI env var, with an explicit escape hatch.
    - **`transform` over `bundle` when the module graph is the contract.** Per-file minification keeps 52 modules as 52 modules and cannot touch a dynamic import whose specifier is a runtime string. Bundling changes what the network does, not just what the bytes say.
    - **Mask before you strip.** A comment regex over HTML will happily eat comment-shaped text inside `<script>` — including JSON-LD. Park the script and style bodies, strip, restore, then assert the JSON still parses.
    - **Check the minifier's defaults for the exact thing you are removing.** esbuild preserves `/*! */` banners and moves them to the end of the file, which would have left one surviving artefact of the precise kind being removed.
    - **Verify a build by running the test suite against its output.** 674 tests against minified code is evidence; reading the minified file is not. It is affordable here only because git holds the original — that is what makes rewriting the real tree an acceptable way to test.
    - **Say what the change does not buy.** Minified is not obfuscated. The scorer and the solver are still readable, and nothing about the security posture moved. Leaving that unsaid invites someone to later trust the client because it "looks compiled".
95. **The player was choosing their own hand** (§11am).
    - **A comment that asserts a security property is the reason nobody checks it.** "The one thing the player cannot forge" had been at the top of the verifier since §11z, and it was never true. The rest of that design is sound; it was built on one sentence that was assumed rather than established.
    - **Write-once is not unforgeable.** The column grants genuinely prevented a reroll and were cited as the proof of safety. The first write is the only one an attacker wants, and nothing constrained it. Ask which write matters, not whether writes are restricted.
    - **A pure function is a symmetric asset.** `dealHand` being deterministic is what lets the server re-derive an honest run *and* what lets an attacker pre-select a dishonest one. Anything that makes verification cheap makes search cheap.
    - **Separate the vulnerability from the amplifier before fixing either.** A client-chosen seed is the hole; an unbounded `play_date` is what turned one grind into a year of rigged days. The screenshot made the amplifier look like the bug, and closing only that leaves the exploit intact at one request per night.
    - **Overwrite rather than reject when you can.** Replacing the client's seed means no request shape gets a chosen value through and no client change is needed — a validator only ever refuses the inputs you thought of.
    - **A silent rewrite of a value the client reads back is a lockout.** Pinning `play_date` looked equivalent to rejecting it; the client queries by the date it sent, so storing a different one makes the next load claim again and collide. Reject loudly, and leave the value alone.
    - **Two clocks disagree at the boundary, which is also the peak.** An exact date match would turn seconds of ordinary skew into a failed claim at 19:00 New York. The tolerance is safe *only* because the seed is now server-minted — the two decisions are coupled, not independent.
    - **Exempt privileged roles or a migration's own self-test will corrupt real rows.** 022 inserts a 1999 date and cleans up by that date; a hard pin would have written a live row for today against a real account and then lost track of it.
    - **A step that exists only in a comment is a step that does not happen.** 015's commented-out revokes were run in the end, but the repo could not say so — intent is recorded, action is not. Where the operation is safe (deleting unplayed rows is what `admin_reset_day` already does), make the migration do it and report counts.
    - **Becoming the attacker is the only self-test worth writing.** Proving the trigger *exists* is true of the broken version too. The check has to `set local role authenticated`, set the JWT claim RLS reads, and try the real seed — without both, it passes for the wrong reason.
    - **State the weakness you did not close.** The cleanup deletes future rows before looking for duplicate seeds, so a lone surviving rigged row would slip through. It worked here because of where the game day happened to fall — a fact about the calendar, not about the query.
    - **A trust flag stored inside untrusted data is decoration.** `verified` sits in the same jsonb a forger would be writing. Its presence only proves they did not think to set it; provenance has to live where the client cannot reach.
    - **Audit one thing, find another.** Double or Nothing had never been resolved server-side: the multiplier was 1 on every wager day, paying busted wagers in full and quietly robbing won ones. It was mis-scoring honest players, in both directions, and no cheat was involved.
    - **A failing test can be the test being wrong.** The busted-wager assertion expected 0 and got 150, because Pity Points are added after the multiplier. The code was right; the assertion encoded a rule the game does not have.
94. **Stack the deck** (§11al).
    - **Server-authoritative scoring makes every "just change what's dealt" feature a two-sided change.** Rigging the browser alone would have shown a Royal Flush and paid for whatever the seed really dealt. Ask what else derives the same value before overriding it in one place.
    - **Determinism is the contract, and breaking it fails only in production.** The board and the Edge Function agree because every un-pinned card comes from the row's own seed. A `freshSeed()` in there passes locally and rejects every real rigged run — so the test is the property (*same seed and stack ⇒ identical deal*), not a case.
    - **A table-level RLS policy publishes columns that do not exist yet.** `using (result is not null)` on `daily_plays` means any new column is readable by every signed-in player — so "which runs were rigged" would have been public. §11ac's lesson, met from the other direction: this time the column was the new thing, not the policy.
    - **Do not let the client consume its own rigging.** The claim is a browser INSERT; a patched page skips whatever step you put there. An `after insert` trigger runs inside the same statement and cannot be opted out of.
    - **Queued is editable, attached is frozen.** Rewriting cards a player may be looking at is precisely the desync the whole design exists to prevent — so the write path simply cannot reach an attached row.
    - **Refuse a malformed stack; do not repair it.** Dropping an unknown rarity to null changes the score silently. Five-cards-exactly matters because `evaluateHand` throws and it runs in the render path.
    - **Make both halves fail the same way.** A bad stack deals normally in the browser and is refused by the server: different responses, one premise, so they can never score different cards.
    - **Parallelise the read nobody needs.** The stacked deal is fetched alongside the claim, so a feature that is almost never active costs nothing on the path every player takes every day.
    - **`nth(-1)` is the LAST element, not "nothing".** A `findIndex` that found no enabled card clicked the wrong one and produced a confident, wrong failure. Guard the -1 before using it.
    - **Face-up is not interactive.** Cards finish flipping a beat before they can be clicked, and a fixed `waitForTimeout` straddles that gap differently on every run. Wait for the condition, and make it the CONJUNCTION of every property you actually need.
    - **The day's modifier still applies to a rigged hand.** Locked Card locked the pinned 2♣ — correct, and surprising enough to be worth writing down before someone assumes the stack is broken.
    - **Deploy order is a real dependency when two deployables share a function.** Migration, then Edge Function, then queue a deal. Put that in the migration's success notice, not only in the design doc.
    - **A trigger inside someone else's transaction can end their day.** `attach_stacked_deal` ran in the INSERT that claims the seed, so a collision on the rigging table aborted the claim and locked the player out entirely. Anything that piggybacks on a critical write must fail open — and the collision was reached by a *normal* admin workflow (queue, claim, reset the day, queue again), not by abuse.
    - **A structural self-test cannot tell a fixed function from a broken one.** 021 asserted the trigger existed and was `security definer`; both were true of the version that was breaking play. Where the whole claim is "this does not raise", the test has to actually run it.
    - **Pick the interface from the question being asked, not from the data you happen to have.** An ordered draw pile was the natural shape of the deck; "what do they get if they throw THIS away" is the question. The first version required predicting how many cards a player discards and in what order, which is unknowable — so it was wrong for its only purpose while being perfectly correct code.
    - **When a stored shape changes meaning, retire the old rows — do not reinterpret them.** An ordered list and a slot map are both arrays of cards, so old rows parse cleanly and deal the right cards to the wrong slots. Silent and plausible beats loud and wrong every time, in the bad direction.
    - **Two halves agreeing is not the same as neither punishing the player.** A malformed stack made the board deal normally and the server REFUSE — they never scored different cards, which is what the comment claimed, and the player still lost their run to an admin row they never saw. Check both properties, not just the one you wrote down.
    - **Changing what a shared step does means finding every copy of it first.** "Which card lands where" existed four times. Extracting it was not cleanup done alongside the feature; it was the prerequisite for the feature being correct at all.
    - **Prove the extraction is a no-op before trusting it.** `applyDiscards` without pins had to be byte-identical to four hand-written copies. That equivalence is the test that protects every ordinary hand ever dealt.
    - **A wrapped flex row strands whatever sits between two groups.** The `→` between the dealt card and its replacement ended each first line pointing at nothing. Lead the second group with it instead, and give each block a rule, or "which replacement belongs to which card" is genuinely ambiguous.
93. **The run that was lost by looking at the leaderboard** (§11ak).
    - **A value that exists only inside an in-flight request does not exist.** The submission was un-awaited (correctly — the reveal should not wait on a cold start), which meant a finished run had exactly one copy and a navigation could cancel it. Write the local mirror first; that is what makes fire-and-forget safe rather than lucky.
    - **Ten seconds of animation with a clickable header is not an edge case.** The window for "navigate away mid-submit" is as wide as the reveal, and every header button is a full page load. Ask what is clickable while an async write is outstanding.
    - **A state that means two things needs a tiebreaker, not a better guess.** `result: null` is both "not finished" and "the submission never landed", and the board only knew the first. A mirror keyed to the same seed is what tells them apart.
    - **Compare seeds as numbers.** It round-trips through JSON one way and a Postgres `bigint` the other, and PostgREST may return a string. A strict `!==` fails CLOSED — the recovery silently never fires and looks exactly like a feature that was never built.
    - **`Number()` is too generous to be an equality test.** It maps null, undefined and `''` all to 0, so two ABSENT seeds compare equal and a run with no provenance gets replayed onto an unrelated hand. The test caught it; the first implementation had it.
    - **A retry that succeeded looks like a rejected cheat.** 409 covered both "no hand claimed" and "already finished", and treating the pair as fatal is precisely what made a dropped submission unrecoverable. Read the row back and let the stored result answer.
    - **An unknown identity must not default to a known one — twice now** (§11i was the first). `getSession()` collapsing "unreachable" into "signed out" is harmless for chrome and catastrophic for anything that DEALS A HAND: it re-dealt from a fresh local seed while the account hand sat on the server, silently.
    - **A fallback whose remedy is "play something else" is not a fallback.** "A Supabase hiccup shouldn't block the game" was the right instinct wired to the wrong action. When we know the player and know their hand exists, refusing to deal is the honest move.
    - **Fixing a hang with an await introduces a hang.** The first recovery had no timeout and left the page on "Loading today's hand…" forever — a worse failure than the bug, shipped inside the fix. Found by a browser test; a happy-path assertion would have passed.
    - **On timeout, keep the evidence.** The mirror is retained and the player falls through to where they already were. Same rule as §11w's "don't destroy the evidence on a failure you didn't diagnose".
    - **Copy that guesses the user's state will guess wrong at the worst moment.** "These boards aren't public yet. Log in" was shown to a signed-in player whose SDK failed — the one sentence they meet while hunting a missing score, and it is about the wrong problem.
    - **Blocked storage reproduces the whole report with no network fault at all.** Each quiet degrade in persistence.js is right on its own; together they make a game that deals a new hand on every load and remembers nothing. Probe once and say so.
    - **"I couldn't recreate it" is information about the window, not about the bug.** It needs a click during a specific few seconds against a slow request. Build the harness that can hold the request open instead of trying to be fast enough by hand.
92. **Friend-request notification badge** (§11ai).
    - **A notification that only renders where the user cannot see it is not a notification.** The whole nav collapses behind ☰ under the mobile breakpoint, so a badge on the Friends button alone is invisible on phones — and looks perfect in every desktop screenshot. Ask where the element actually is at each breakpoint before deciding it is done.
    - **Reaching for the existing function is not the same as reaching for the right one.** `getPendingRequests().length` is correct and costs two rowful queries plus a profile join, to render one digit. A `head: true` count returns the number and no rows.
    - **A theme-aware token is not automatically the right token.** `--red` is a FOREGROUND colour, lightened in dark mode to sit on a dark background. Inverted into a fill under white text it fails AA at 3.37:1. Ask what a colour was tuned FOR, not just what it is named.
    - **Measure contrast against the surface the element is actually on.** The first replacement passed at 3.35:1 against the page background and failed at 2.96:1 against the header's own `--surface`, which is what the badge really sits on. Same colour, same page, two different answers, and only one of them is the one a person sees.
    - **A colour constrained from both directions has less room than it looks.** Darker raises text contrast and sinks the pill into the header; lighter does the reverse. Both bounds have to be solved together, which is a search, not a guess.
    - **Refresh on the events that change the answer, not on a clock.** Session change, panel close, and return-to-tab cover every way the count moves; a poll would add load to a game that gives out one hand a day.
    - **Refresh on panel CLOSE, not per action.** One query covers a whole session of accepting and declining, and it makes a stale count behind a now-empty panel impossible.
    - **Fail closed, and let the failure reach the thing that decides.** The query throws like everything else in friends.js; the header turns that into "no badge". Swallowing it in the query would put the policy in the wrong place and make the not-configured test meaningless.
    - **A visual cap and an accessible one are different numbers.** The pill says `9+` to stay a circle; the accessible name says fifteen, because a screen reader has no layout problem to solve.
91. **The wild was named by the card it was dealt as** (§3ab).
    - **A branch that is correct because nothing reaches it is a bug waiting for a schema change.** `item.rarity === 'joker'` was right for years, then §3x moved wildness onto the card and a modern wild started falling through to the rare-card branch — which appends the card's own rank. Nothing about the code changed; what flowed through it did.
    - **The same legacy check had already been found and fixed 200 lines below, with a comment explaining it.** Fixing one instance and not sweeping for the rest is how the second one survives. Grep the shape, not the file.
    - **Two bugs from the same check, and only one was visible.** The earlier instance lost a 🃏 marker, which shows immediately. This one printed a *wrong rank next to a right one*, which needs a wild that actually substituted into a scoring hand before anyone can see it. The less visible instance is the one that survives a sweep.
    - **A player-facing name and the proof under it are two renderings of one thing.** They were built from different sources — the raw card and the logical hand — so they could disagree, and did, two lines apart. Derive both from one value and the class of bug disappears.
    - **Assert the agreement, not the value.** "The label says 5♠" pins today's substitution search; "the label names the same card its own proof strip highlights" is the defect stated directly and survives the search picking a different suit tomorrow.
    - **Deleting a module to prove a test is red proves nothing.** Stashing the whole file made the test fail to *import*, which any test would. Revert the logic and keep the export, or the RED check is theatre.
    - **A fallback must not fall back to the value you are removing.** For a row with no stored logical hand, defaulting to `finalHand` hands a wild exactly the dealt card this fix exists to stop showing. Recompute it, or show nothing — a rankless wild is honest, a wrong rank is not.
    - **Where a card has no substitution, it should claim none.** A discarded wild never played as anything, and its index points into a different array entirely; naming it "Wild" with no rank is the whole answer.
90. **The Skill meter measured the draw, not the decision** (§3aa).
    - **A metric can be wrong for years while every number it prints looks plausible.** `actualScore / bestEV` returns a percentage, moves when play changes, and correlates weakly with skill. Nothing about any single reading looks off. Only measuring the distribution — perfect play scoring a median of 8% — shows it.
    - **Dividing a realised outcome by an expected value is a category error, and an odds-proportional score table makes it a large one.** The EV is dragged upward by rare enormous categories by design, so it sits nowhere near the median outcome and the ratio can essentially never reach 1 by playing well.
    - **Two dials driven by the same variable are one dial.** Skill and Luck both keyed on `actualScore`, which is exactly why the owner's screenshot showed 100% and 100% together. Independence has to be a property of the definitions, not an intention.
    - **Test the property, not the cases.** "The rating does not depend on what was drawn" is the defect stated directly and survives any future re-tuning; a table of input/output pairs would have passed against the broken formula too.
    - **The right measurement was already in the repo, with a comment explaining why it was right.** personality.js documented the discard percentile as the correct signal for finding the optimal play and contrasted it with Decision Rating. Knowing a thing in one module is not the same as using it in another — derive both from one function and they cannot drift.
    - **Grading an outcome against the player's own expectation rewards low expectations.** Luck measured the draw against `chosenEV`, so a deliberately bad discard lowered the bar and inflated the meter. Grade against the field, not against what the player settled for.
    - **A calibration script that does not deal the way the game deals is measuring a different game.** This one used `createDeck`/`shuffle` instead of `dealHand` and so had never seen a wild card — the exact cards that form the top of the distribution it was fitting. Its ceiling was off by a factor of five and nothing failed.
    - **Fit a distribution with a distribution.** One ceiling divided into a heavy-tailed quantity squashes most of the range and saturates the rest. Percentile stops are uniform by construction, and they can be re-checked by re-sampling — which is now a test, so the next time the score table moves this goes red instead of quietly wrong.
    - **Deleting a tuned constant beats re-tuning it.** The draw half was a hand-fitted scale; the solver already enumerates every possible draw, so the exact percentile was available for one option's worth of work. Constants go stale, counts do not.
    - **A default on the "we don't know" branch is a claim, and it will be the most flattering one available.** `Number.isFinite(rating) ? rating : 1` showed a full Skill bar for rounds with no decision in them, directly under a line that had been deliberately hidden for saying too much.
    - **Hiding a value stops being honest once there is somewhere to explain it.** Suppressing the Decision Rating was right until the "?" existed; after that it left the panel contradicting itself, since the Skill meter below showed a dash for the same run.
    - **A yardstick has to match the day.** "What a perfect player could get from this deal" is meaningless when the day forbids discarding, and scoring it against the three-discard table told every wager-day player they were dealt bottom-3% cards.
    - **Switch on the rule, not on the behaviour.** Holding pat by choice and being forbidden to discard produce the same discard count and mean opposite things.
    - **Name what a meter cannot see.** Risk counts discarded cards, so a Double or Nothing round reads 0% for the biggest bet in the game. Saying so is better than the alternative sentence, which was "nothing was at stake".
    - **Explain a number with its inputs, not a restatement of it.** "Your play came 18th of the 31 you could legally have made" answers the question; "Skill is how well you played" does not.
    - **Fixing a metric changes everything downstream that consumed it.** The XP term keyed to this rating had been paying about a sixth of its advertised value, achievements named for it were unlocking on luck, and stored rows are on the old unbounded scale and need capping wherever they are still read.
    - **The box model was the thing that agreed with the bug, again.** The "?" measured 2.08px low against `<strong>`'s rect and 0.5px high against the digits' actual ink. Third time in this project that a computed value confirmed the wrong answer and only rendered pixels disagreed.
89. **The EV solve starts at the deal** (§4h).
    - **Measure the frequency before optimising the cost.** The worry was three-plus wilds; that is 0 of 20,000 real deals and only reachable from the admin panel. The case worth fixing was ONE wild, at one run in eleven.
    - **A wait is not a freeze, and they need different fixes.** The worker already kept the main thread under 64ms. No amount of shaving the evaluator addresses six seconds of waiting; moving *when* it happens does.
    - **Work that does not depend on the user's input can start before they give it.** The solve prices every discard option, so the player's choice was never an input — it was just being started at the moment they made one.
    - **Key a cache on its inputs, not on a token.** A token answers "same deal"; only the inputs answer "same question", and `maxDiscards` is reassigned mid-run by three different modifiers.
    - **A shared worker makes speculative work a cost, not a freebie.** Solving early on a day whose rules are still provisional would block the solve that counts behind it.
    - **A differential test has to wait for the thing to finish.** The first run compared count-up values mid-animation and would have called any two runs identical.
88. **Even Money was called that on odd days** (§4g).
    - **When a value becomes parameterised, every string that describes it has to follow.** `describe()` was updated for both halves and `label` was left behind, so the name contradicted the rule underneath it half the time.
    - **A parameterised thing has two labels, not one.** The resolved day names its actual half; the unresolved entry a picker lists cannot, and must not pretend to.
    - **Splitting one modifier into two changes the rotation's odds.** The tempting fix would have doubled how often parity days come up, invisibly.
    - **Test the contradiction, not the cases.** "The label and the description never disagree" is the defect stated directly, and survives a future third variant that two hardcoded assertions would not.
87. **The standing chip ranked the wrong run** (§11ag).
    - **A parameter that is accepted and ignored looks exactly like one that works.** `renderStanding(resultPanel, total)` took a score for two sections' worth of history and never passed it on. The signature was the documentation, and the documentation was wrong.
    - **A plausible wrong number is worse than a broken one.** The chip reported a real placing for a real run, under a completely different score. Nothing about it looked off.
    - **Test mode not persisting is a feature that leaks into anything reading the database.** Any surface that answers "how did I do" by querying saved rows will silently answer about a different run when the run on screen was never saved.
    - **Assert what reaches the wire, not that something rendered.** "A chip appears" passed throughout. "The displayed score arrives at the function that ranks it" is what catches this.
    - **Half a bug report can be a misreading, and that is still worth answering.** "Out of everyone, not just the top 25" was already true — but the chip hides its denominator by design, so the wording earns the confusion.
    - **Never overload a function reached through PostgREST.** It resolves an RPC by the JSON keys in the body, so two signatures whose optional parameters overlap are both candidates and it refuses to pick — `PGRST203`. Add a defaulted parameter instead; an overload kept "for compatibility" is an outage for both versions.
    - **A migration's self-test runs in the wrong place to catch a PostgREST fault.** 018's passed because it called the function from SQL, where Postgres's arity resolution applies. Only an HTTP request through the API sees what clients see.
    - **A feature that fails closed fails silently.** The chip resolves null on any error, so a broken RPC produced no error, no console noise, and no missing element — just an absence nobody would report.
86. **Hand-rankings cheat sheet and first-visit intro** (§11af).
    - **"They didn't understand it" is usually a discovery problem, not a copy problem.** How to Play was already accurate and clear. It was behind an icon nobody clicks, which no amount of rewriting fixes.
    - **Mark a "once ever" flag when you SHOW the thing, not when it is dismissed.** Waiting for a dismissal reopens it for everyone who closed the tab, turning an introduction into a nuisance.
    - **Two questions asked at different moments want two surfaces.** The tutorial is read before the first hand; "does a Flush beat a Straight" is asked mid-decision. One modal serving both means scrolling past the wrong one under time pressure.
    - **A reference that quietly contradicts what its readers already know is a trap.** Four Straight beating Two Pair is a Cardle rule; publishing it unlabelled would mislead precisely the people who need the least help.
    - **Teach and remind from the same string.** `HAND_DESCRIPTIONS` now feeds both the cheat sheet and the score badges, so there is no second definition of a Full House to drift.
    - **A closure that reads no closure state is just a function in the wrong place.** `openHelpModal` needed exporting, not an event bus — unlike §11ac's login modal, which genuinely owns session state.
    - **Auto-opening a panel raises the bar on its copy.** The same How to Play that was fine behind an icon became the product's first impression, and five headings of prose is a wall when nobody asked for it.
    - **Half of "too much text" is measure, not word count.** Widening 420px → 540px did as much as the cuts; short copy still reads as a wall when every line wraps.
    - **Name a collapsed section after the question, not the topic.** "Why is that card glowing?" is what someone actually wonders; "Rare cards" only makes sense once you already know the answer.
    - **Check that a decorative glyph exists in the default font.** `⌄` fell back to something indistinguishable from a lowercase v, so the disclosure control read as a typo.
85. **Google Ads tag** (§11ae).
    - **"Immediately after `<head>`" and "before `<meta charset>`" are not the same instruction.** Charset has to land in the first 1024 bytes; an async tag only needs its request to start early. Following the vendor's wording literally would have spent that budget for nothing.
    - **Test a third-party script with it BLOCKED, not just with it working.** That is the common case for an analytics tag, and the only assertion that matters is that the product is identical without it.
    - **A metric that adblockers can veto is a floor, not a count.** Worth writing down before someone compares it against `daily_plays` and concludes the game is broken.
    - **Record what you deliberately did not build.** Conversion events and an EU consent banner are both absent by decision; undocumented absence reads as an oversight to whoever looks next.
84. **Per-route titles, canonicals and indexing** (§11ad).
    - **A distinct title does nothing while a canonical says the page is somewhere else.** The requested change was the visible half; the hardcoded `rel="canonical"` pointing every route at `/` was the half that decided whether any of it counted.
    - **A decision's stated reason is a tripwire — go back and check it when the code moves.** The one-entry sitemap and the site-wide canonical both explained themselves as "these routes render an empty shell without a session". §11ac made that false the commit before, and nothing pointed at them to say so.
    - **Say what a change does NOT buy.** Titles set by JavaScript reach Google on a delayed second pass and never reach most unfurlers. Shipping them as "SEO done" would have been a quiet overstatement of the result.
    - **More indexed pages is not always the goal.** One page per player is unbounded, stale within a day, and publishes other people's names — the case where the generic advice and the site's interest genuinely diverge.
    - **`Disallow` is not `noindex`.** One asks a crawler not to fetch; a URL it never fetched can still be indexed from inbound links. The tag is what binds.
    - **Absence is a state — prefer removing a tag to setting its opposite.** Building `robots` on demand means no bug in that code path can de-index the homepage, because the homepage never has the tag to get wrong.
83. **Public leaderboards and profiles** (§11ac).
    - **Opening a feature is not the same as opening a table.** Widening the RLS policy to `anon` would have published every column of `daily_plays` — including `seed`, and every column added to it in future — through PostgREST. Elevated functions that enumerate their own output columns expose the feature and nothing else.
    - **A rule that says when to elevate also says when to stop not elevating.** §11j chose `security invoker` because those functions read what the caller already may. That premise expired the moment the caller could be anonymous, so following the same rule produced the opposite answer.
    - **`security definer` moves a safety check out of the policy and into your hands.** `result is not null` is what stops an unfinished hand's seed leaking; RLS was enforcing it in one place, and now four function bodies have to.
    - **Ask what the door already lets through before widening it.** `is_admin` had been world-readable since the first schema, and only looking at the anon surface on purpose surfaced it.
    - **A control with two indistinguishable states is worse than no control.** Global/Friends signed out would toggle between the same board, so it is hidden; Add Friend has somewhere real to go, so it stays.
    - **The negative assertion is the one that matters for a read-only view.** "The profile renders" proves little; "no sign-out, delete, rename or customise control exists anywhere on it" is the test.
82. **Multi-wild hands were solved by hill-climbing** (§3z).
    - **A greedy search cannot cross a valley.** Improving one card at a time from five 2s never reaches a Royal Flush, because every intermediate hand is worse than the Four of a Kind it starts on. Where the optimum needs several simultaneous changes, "improve one thing at a time" is not an approximation of the answer — it is a different answer.
    - **"Bounded and not provably optimal" was a comment, not a measurement.** It was written to justify the shortcut and nobody checked how far off it landed. The gap was 234,078 against 54,800,000.
    - **A test that asserts TERMINATION asserts nothing about correctness.** "Five wilds resolve without hanging" passed for the entire life of the bug.
    - **Look for the invariant, not just the reported case.** "More wilds can never score less" is one line, would have caught three, four and five wilds at once, and is checkable on random hands forever. The bug report named only five.
    - **Fix the count above the one reported.** Four wilds was broken identically and unnoticed; stopping at the reported symptom would have shipped half a fix.
    - **Collapsing a dimension can make brute force affordable.** Suits only matter through `isFlush`, so all wilds want one suit — which turns 13⁵ permutations into 6,188 combinations and makes the exhaustive answer cheaper than the greedy guess.
    - **Measure the fix against the bug before claiming a cost.** The exhaustive search is FASTER here than the hill-climb it replaced. The seconds in a multi-wild solve were pre-existing, in a branch this change never touched.
    - **A frozen score and a derived label will eventually contradict each other.** §11o chose to derive hand names from cards precisely so they could not drift, and this fix made old rows drift the other way — the name now improves while the points do not.
81. **Click-to-view hand breakdowns** (§11ab).
    - **Check whether the design doc's premise is still true before trusting a security decision built on it.** This feature is only safe because deals are per-player now; §2 still said they were shared, and the whole gate was scoped against a rule the code stopped following. §11w had fixed the same false claim in two other places without tracing it home.
    - **A purity boundary pays a third time.** §11z used it to let the server import the real scorer. Here it lets a *UI* module be lifted out of board.js and unit-tested in Node — no DOM, no timers, so the thing three surfaces now depend on has direct coverage instead of only browser tests.
    - **When two callers want opposite orderings, own the flip in one place.** `buildScoreBadges` is ascending for the reveal animation; every static caller wants descending. A second `.reverse()` at a second call site is a bug that renders perfectly and is simply upside down.
    - **Send it later, not wider.** The board could have returned each row's full result; that is 25 several-KB blobs per tab switch to serve the one a player might open. Same trade §11aa made in the opposite direction.
    - **Put the control on the thing, not the row.** The row already held a link to somewhere else. One activatable region with two destinations is unresolvable from a keyboard.
    - **Promoting an element to a button is a layout change.** The grid alignment §11n fought for is exactly what a `<span>`→`<button>` swap threatens, so it got measured against a 54,800,000 score and a long username rather than eyeballed.
    - **A fixture trimmed to what today's callers read will break tomorrow's.** The fake account held five score fields because five were enough; a new caller walked all twenty and threw. The guard caught it, which is the point of the guard — but a dev tool that renders nothing is not moving.
    - **Screenshot both themes.** The discarded-card fade that read as clearly-secondary on light was barely legible on dark. No assertion was ever going to say so.
    - **A derived stat that records WHICH usually needs to record WHEN.** `bestRun` kept the run and dropped its date, so the first caller that wanted to label it was one reference-equality trick away from a bug. Cheaper to widen the stat than to reconstruct the context at every call site.
    - **Test the near-miss, not the happy path.** `bestRunDate` is only meaningfully wrong when the best run isn't the most recent one — so that is the fixture, and a passing test on three ascending scores would have proven nothing.
    - **Check whether the blocker needs code before writing any.** Sign-in was unreachable on localhost, which looked like a dev-tooling gap. `verifyOtp` is origin-independent, so swapping the host in the emailed link was the entire fix.
80. **Daily standing** (§11aa).
    - **Count where the data already is.** The browser version of this downloads every player's full result to produce one integer, and leaks all their hands doing it. Two integers came back instead.
    - **A percentage of a tiny sample is noise wearing a suit.** Below a real field the chip says nothing rather than something that changes hourly for reasons the player did not cause.
    - **Say the shorter half.** "TOP 90%" is a euphemism; "BOTTOM 10%" is the same fact and actually informative. Colour by how well you did, not by which word the label picked, or adjacent placings look like opposites.
    - **A geometry assertion that only compares `top` to `top` proves almost nothing.** It passed while the chip overlapped the badge above it. Comparing against the other element's `bottom` is what "below" actually means.
    - **Screenshot the thing.** Fifteen passing browser assertions did not notice two pills sitting on top of each other; one look did.
79. **Server-authoritative scoring** (§11z).
    - **A purity boundary is a security asset, not just a testing one.** Because `src/core/` was never allowed to touch the network or the DOM, the server-side verifier imports the real scorer instead of reimplementing it. The version of this fix that duplicates the scoring rules is worse than no fix, because the copy drifts.
    - **Find the input the attacker does not control and rebuild from it.** The seed was already write-once and already sufficient; the missing piece was somewhere to run the check, not more data.
    - **Verify the direction nobody thinks about.** The important test is not "does it catch a forged score" but "does it reproduce the honest one exactly" — a mismatch there silently rewrites every legitimate player's total. 300 random seeds, byte-identical.
    - **Bound what is expensive to verify instead of trusting it.** Re-running the EV solve per submission would dominate the backend's cost to protect 200 points out of a possible 54,800,000. Accept it, cap it, and say so.
    - **Ship a fallback so deploy ORDER cannot break anything.** The client keeps the old write path while the function is absent; the migration that removes it is commented out and gated behind a query proving the new path works.
78. **Security, performance and correctness audit** (§11y).
    - **An omitted `WITH CHECK` defaults to `USING`, which is almost never what you meant.** It only asks "is the new row still yours", not "is the change one you may make" — which let the addressee of a request rewrite who supposedly sent it.
    - **A deploy config can undo a database design.** The blocklist was unreadable by RLS and world-readable as a `.sql` file. Ask what the static root actually serves, not what you think you published.
    - **A comment saying "nothing here is untrusted" is a place to look, not a reason not to.** It was true when written and false when the word bank became admin-editable; it then discouraged everyone from checking the one unescaped interpolation in the file.
    - **Admin-authored content is untrusted content.** Not because admins are hostile, but because "one compromised admin becomes JavaScript in every session" is a real escalation path, and validating the value beats trusting six render sites to remember.
    - **Two functions answering the same question will eventually disagree.** The scorer and the proof strip broke run ties in opposite directions, so the player was shown one hand and paid for another. Derive both from one function or pin the agreement with a test.
    - **A special mode that removes the choice also removes the thing you were measuring.** Double or Nothing made every skill metric degenerate to a perfect score; the fix is to report "not measured", because zero would be as false as full marks.
    - **Check whether an optimisation was applied to every branch.** The one-wild search pruned suits; the two-wild search right below it did not, and that branch dominated the cost.
    - **Differential-test a behaviour-preserving change against the code it replaces.** "The suite still passes" is weaker than "4,000 random hands produce byte-identical output".
    - **Verify a reported vulnerability before fixing it, and after.** The repo-exposure finding first probed as a 308 redirect and looked wrong; following the redirect showed 200 and 392 KB.
    - **Probe the deployed function, not just the migration's own self-test.** The self-test passed — its cases simply did not include a name whose digits collapsed it into an exact entry. Calling the live RPC with a dozen realistic names found in seconds what the fixture had no reason to cover.
    - **A refusal should not repeat the word back.** It confirms a list meant to be unreadable, it insults the person who tripped a false positive, and it teaches evasion. "Not available" answers all three.
    - **An availability check that gives a REASON is an enumeration endpoint.** One boolean, with "blocked" and "taken" deliberately indistinguishable.
    - **Fail open on an advisory check.** The trigger enforces; the pre-check only warns. Failing closed would have let one CDN hiccup block every new sign-up.
    - **Deleting the account was the only moderation tool, which meant there was none.** A rename is the proportionate response to a bad name; without it the choice was ignore it or destroy someone's history.
    - **Put the test where the code runs.** The matching rules cannot be exercised from Node, so the migration self-tests on execution — including the false-positive cases, which are the ones a word filter gets wrong.
    - **Marketing copy is a claim about the system, and nothing type-checks it.** The meta description asserted a mechanic that had been deliberately removed, and shipped, because meta tags are the one part of a page nobody looks at while developing. The same stale premise was in How to Play. Describe behaviour by reading the code that implements it, not the feature you remember.
    - **A stub that blocks one origin no longer means offline — the second time this bit.** The fallback CDN added last batch left seven more suites silently testing a REACHABLE backend; `config-run`'s "works with config completely unavailable" was the one that failed loudly. Matching both origins with a RegExp says what was actually meant.
